The Atlantic
What Were My Dreams Trying to Tell Me?
2026-09-21

What Were My Dreams Trying to Tell Me?

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
When I was 20 years old, I started to sense a hole inside me. The feeling was utterly concrete, though the result of no medical condition that any of the various doctors I saw could identify. The hole felt like a viscous emptiness—a paradox, admittedly, as any real emptiness should have felt like nothing. This was a nothing, but its nothingness very much felt like something—and something that was growing. Check out more from this issue and find your next story to read. Accompanying this feeling of an expanding hole at the center of me was a sense that I was losing more and more of myself to it, like shoreline eroded by rising waters. To lose myself felt less like oblivion than instability. I observed in myself a new susceptibility to the opinions of others, and a new recklessness, as if some dark, decisive action might either quiet my doubts about my fundamental worth or end my longing once and for all. I recall one particularly dangerous, drunken night that had me well over the legal limit, both aware that I was getting into a car that could kill me and others, and also not believing that any of it mattered. The onset of my devolving well-being was strange and sudden, and seemed connected to childhood in some way I couldn’t understand. I hadn’t been a tortured child but rather placid, if introverted. My parents always said that I had been easy to keep happy. It sometimes seemed to me as if the hole I was experiencing was one where some piece of my childhood had once resided. My friends found my behavior disturbing. I was missing class in college, showing up to theater rehearsals drunk. A caring young woman I was dating could take no more of my savage, ranting mood swings and persistent despair. She broke up with me over tea at the student union and implored me to seek help. I knew she was right. Even sleep was now fraught with violent, terrifying dreams—of rocket ships out of control, conflagrations and invasions, and, yes, holes into which I would disappear. But exactly what sort of help I might need, I didn’t know. I confided my woes to a friend who’d grown up on a farm in Kentucky. He never missed Sunday Mass, and when I told him about the hole inside me, he pulled a copy of The Seven Storey Mountain from his bag and handed it to me. Thomas Merton, he said, had written about something similar. It was what had led him to leave the world and become a monk. I found deep resonance in the book’s harrowing account of Merton’s time at Cambridge and Columbia—the intense restlessness and emptiness, the disgust and torpor and despair. But Merton’s solution was theistic: a turn to God that I could only read as naive and distorting. I didn’t have any interest in God. Even the passages that approached the specificity of my own experience were charged with personification that read as anachronistic at best: The room was very quiet. It was rather dark, too. And as I lay in bed, in my weariness and pain and disgust, I felt for a moment the shadow of another visitor pass into the room. It was death, that came to stand by my bed. I kept my eyes closed, more out of apathy than anything else. But anyway, there was no need to open one’s eyes to see the visitor, to see death. Death is someone you see very clearly with eyes in the center of your heart: eyes that see not by reacting to light, but by reacting to a kind of a chill from within the marrow of your own life. I knew this experience: a central chill, a lightlessness connected to the doomed feeling of some ultimate end. But I resisted any characterization that departed from the fundamental sensation itself—of a growing hole inside me. For no reason I knew, I was convinced that remaining honest with myself about this basic fact was the only thing I could do to help myself. My parents were worried. They flew out to visit me at school and figure out what was wrong. Over a meal they prepared for me in a hotel room with a kitchen, I confided in them. There was a hole inside me that I needed to address, I said. They were understandably perplexed. My mother found me a psychiatrist, an avuncular Brit who was more interested in my sex life than my interior void, which I insisted was more real than he seemed willing to allow. But something important endured from the handful of sessions I had with him. A volume on the shelf over his left ear drew my fascination, and would soon fill weeks of my life: Freud’s The Interpretation of Dreams. That book began a process in me, what I’ve often called “curiosity” when I’ve told this story, but which is probably better described as a form of relationship. The nightly tumult of disturbing dreams persisted, and even if I couldn’t be sure whether Freud was right about how to make sense of them, simply attempting to do so seemed to help. Paying attention made it easier to remember my dreams, and to remember more of them. In one, I was carefully drawing an empty circle on a page of the Quran that my grandparents had given me as a boy, a circle through which—impossibly—I could reach and touch cool water. I awoke with the feeling that the hole inside me might be leading to something better, however unknown. Even so, it wasn’t until I mentioned that I was interested in dreams to a teacher I admired that I started to keep a detailed nightly log. This teacher had done a great deal of dream-work, and suggested that I tie a pencil to my finger to remind me, in those brief moments of stirring between sleep cycles, to write down whatever fragments were stirring in hypnagogic consciousness. So I did. What resulted was a daily haul of interior riches. The vividness of my unadulterated hopes and sorrows, the unusual expansiveness and wonder I experienced by night, lived on in the often-paradoxical images I would recall throughout the day. My study of Freud led to a similar scouring of the work of other psychoanalysts—and, later, mystics such as Emanuel Swedenborg, Ibn Arabi, and Rabbi Nachman—for clues about what kind of sense to be making of my dreams. In my final year of college, I encountered, in a late work of Carl Jung’s, the advice to treat dreams as meaningful psychic events, a technique to facilitate what Jung called their “compensatory” function—balancing the biases and limitations of one’s conscious attitudes. I took this to mean treating a dream’s images as real in their own right. Whether doing so had any such balancing effect on me, I can’t be sure. But my persistent despair seemed to be dimming; the hole inside me felt different the more I was able to gaze patiently into it for whatever was coming through. The dream-work was paying dividends, though what exactly was paying them out, I had no clue. I know how what I’m about to share is likely to come off; it isn’t the first time I’ve spoken it aloud. I started to dream of things that hadn’t happened yet, to “see” things at night that then appeared in my life during the day. One of the earliest and most startling of these uncanny doublings took place in my first year after college. I was living and working in a small town in the Tuscan countryside, without a phone, before the internet. I dreamed that a friend I knew in Paris showed up on my doorstep with skis on his back. Two days later, I awoke to the sound of my name being called from down in the piazza, outside my front window. I opened the worn balcony doors to find the very friend I’d dreamed of; he’d gotten my Italian address from a mutual friend and seen that the town I was living in wasn’t too far out of the way as he made the trip from Rome up to the city in the Alps where he planned to spend a few days skiing. I resisted speaking of what had happened, lest I be impelled to name whatever in me had seized my bewildered attention. The Tuscan episode was not the last time dream and day seemed to cross wires. Years later, I would dream of a terrorist attack on Manhattan two days before 9/11, and of a malign, cough-inducing fog slowly engulfing the world several weeks before the eruption of the coronavirus into my (and the world’s) awareness—improbable coincidences I struggled to accept as such. And yet, these visions became the occasion to doubt any picture of the universe that failed to make room for what could only be experienced as a kind of magic. If the prognosticating mode had been the only benefit to accrue from my attention to my dreams, I might not have been so diligent in my recordkeeping. After all, I wasn’t seeing stock prices or lottery numbers, the only rationale many might have accepted for my habit of destroying every good night’s rest. For at that point, I was waking up every 45 minutes to reach for the bedside pad on which I made notes about whatever was moving around inside me. But my night work was opening up creative vistas. One mentor, well-versed in working with the unconscious, suggested that it was enlarging the space of what she kept calling—despite my confusion about what she really meant by it—my psyche. Indeed, a growing spaciousness was now my sense of the interior core where the hole had opened up. The hole itself wasn’t gone, but it had changed character, less a wound now than an opening, though I couldn’t always tell the difference. And yet, the trajectory seemed to be shifting. That pliant, receptive brightness bearing the imprint of something more than just my name, the self, began to feel heightened, enlarged. I had learned that some cultures conceived of the self as containing two parts: a smaller, individual, daily self, and a larger, multiple, primordial one. This greater self, it was believed, was the one that dreamed. Something about this way of thinking felt more useful—and more reflective of my experience—than any of the latest studies in neuroscience I read about the physiological functions of dreams. Psyche : Greek for “soul.” Which felt like a more appropriate frame of reference—despite my continued resistance to the language of theism—for experiences that so evidently defied the materialist doctrines of my late-20th-century education. To privilege the Newtonian model of classical mechanics—one object pushing or pulling another, all the way down to the molecules that formed consciousness—over the actual data of my experience felt like intellectual laziness. I understood that others would see my willingness to abide more patiently in the mystery as irrational. My usage of the Greek term started to give way to its English corollary. The word soul seemed to make better sense of whatever was expanding inside me, a rising that was also a deepening, married to the uncanny knowing of what seemed like prognostication, and, more valuably, to a natural empathy for others. And in case this comes off as more altruistic than seems reasonable: Less blinded by myself, more porous to others, I found, too, that I was becoming a better writer. From the November 2024 issue: Jeffrey Goldberg, Ayad Akhtar, Robert Downey Jr., and Bartlett Sher on writing in the age of AI Soul was, for me then, a mode of being mostly experienced in images by night that shaped my understanding by day—for example, that a close friend was like the abandoned dog I dreamed he was, long neglected by his family, never allowed into the house; an image that helped me navigate his touchiness, providing readier access to the wellspring of loving loyalty that coursed beneath his habitual choler. I found in the use of soul a capaciousness that not only accounted for this richer relationship to others and the world—and to myself—but served as a bridge to deeper mystery, and to a new patience that allowed me to acclimate to it. There were other benefits, too. Just as the vital, vivid stuff of my brain’s nightlife enriched my being—to use a word that joined soul in the lexicon of descriptive terms of dubious provenance—so did my immersion in dream dross seem to feed my capacities with language. There was a stronger tether now between word and thing, a tether, paradoxically, that operated simultaneously in many directions and layers. The webbed, protean mode of associative thinking—one thing reaching for another, in opposing vectors at once—was less an effort than a reflex, more instinctive than the ordinal fixity of my usual habits of mind. Shakespeare, whom I’d long struggled to apprehend, now made easier sense, even as the experience of reading him felt like perpetual entry into undiscovered country, the phonetic expatiations, the dense semantic clusters, the sounds and images opening up more and more effortlessly into a particular state of absorptive flow, a pleasing emotional and intellectual static that I took to calling “linguistic space.” I started taking a yoga class on the Upper West Side. Our becalming teacher spent the better part of our weekly hour teaching us to meditate. Setting aside my irritation with her hushed and holy tones, I found myself growing more susceptible to her instructions. I could catch a gap between thoughts that felt curiously similar to a rich blankness that resulted from hours spent in linguistic space. The fixed point of focus on the breath sharpened my mind and filled it with something closer to light than thought. On my own, I began to sit—sometimes for hours—in this pregnant blankness, exhibiting a facility for what the yoga teacher called “practice” that I couldn’t explain. My weekly hour with that yoga teacher sparked an interest in meditation that led to weeks, and years, of working with other instructors of inner techniques. I learned to visualize and hold in memory shapes derived from Gnostic practices and colors derived from Sufi ones. Intoning Abulafia’s list of Hebrew names for God at the proper pitch and volume shook the bones in my ear and nerves along my neck. Calm was promised, and often followed, though I wondered whether this said more about my susceptibility to suggestion than the practices themselves. Sometimes what followed was stranger. Once, as I sat in a darkened room at dusk, observing the breath going in and out of me, I felt that breath begin to breathe itself. In the half shadows, with my eyes closed, a startling brightness waxed and waned with the movement of air. What was this light? I wondered. Was this just the room’s faint light filtering through my eyelids, brightening and dimming as my pupils dilated and contracted with each breath? If so, was this suggestion too? I would come to no definitive answer. The inner light continued to strobe. If it was all suggestion, I didn’t mind. It was getting me further than faith had. From Meister Eckhart I learned to recognize in my meditative blankness a silence clean of longing, one that, if I could remain in it, would release an astonishing vitality. Others I studied with found it strange that my interest in meditation would be piqued by dream-work and quickened by pentameter. They sometimes remarked that what I was up to resembled anthropology more than devotion. They were only partly right. To me, it was all a study in being, or soul, or spirit (the latest addition to my lexicon)—which is to say, an inquiry into the substance and nature of the interior life. My reading, too, reflected the scavenging of a magpie in search of material from which to build its nest: the Phaedrus and Patanjali and Mulla Sadra; Being and Time one month, Mathnawi the next. What exactly I thought I was doing was not a matter of concern as long as I kept to myself about it. What I’m compressing into paragraphs here took so much longer, of course, and was hardly as sequential as telling makes it sound. I kept at it for the reward of the work itself. I had experiences of rapture (and sorrow) so deep, of pleasures so unprecedented, it was sometimes hard to return to the dishes and the rent. But I had studied with a remarkable Vedic teacher who warned me about seeking states rather than the truth. “What is the truth?” I once asked him as we sauntered along a Tennessee creek. He pulled his orange robe tighter against a rising spring wind. He was too smart to oblige. “The truth is not like knowledge about that tree,” he said, pointing to a blooming magnolia. “Only the tree knows its truth.” I didn’t want to pretend I understood him. “You write stories,” he said. “Inside the story is a meaning that is the reason for it. Not words that carry it, but the wordless substance of its truth you take away.” Despite my customary reticence about such things, I had occasions to compare notes with fellow travelers. One evening, at a fundraising function downtown, I found myself sharing martinis with a physician whose lived-in eloquence about his Zen practice felt like an opening. I was not expecting the lesson in brain science he proceeded to impart: I’d become addicted, he said, to activating certain parts of my cerebral cortex. Like an addict—I flinched at that word—I was pulling on neurological switches, training my brain to release its own pleasure chemicals through a growing grab bag of inner tricks. Clearly he didn’t approve. I was too embarrassed to ask why, even as I recalled my teacher’s warning not to chase states, but rather to seek the wordless substance of truth. I resisted the physician’s account even as I couldn’t ignore the unease it stirred in me. My fellow traveler was right, at least as far as neuroscience went. Studies were beginning to show that meditation—a loose term for the various practices of inner focus I was involved with—activated and deactivated specific parts of the human brain. In the 1990s, the neuroscientist Andrew Newberg studied advanced Tibetan monks and Franciscan nuns as they entered deep meditative states. The results were strikingly similar. In both groups, areas of the brain believed to be responsible for orienting us in space and time showed markedly decreased activity, as if the neural basis for the boundary between self and other had gone quiet. They were using very different techniques, but the results, neurologically speaking, were nearly indistinguishable. Each group, however, described the experience differently: the nuns as a feeling of union with Christ, the monks as a state of emptiness. I knew enough about Buddhism to recognize in that word, emptiness, something very different from the hole that had opened up inside me during early adulthood. For the Buddhist, perception of the nature of reality discloses the identity of emptiness with form, and form with emptiness. It’s a perception described in other traditions by other means, and sometimes, in Christianity, as an annihilation of the individual personality in the presence of God. The maps and words and techniques were different; somehow, the underlying experience itself, beyond words or ideas, was the same. Not quite in the words of my Vedic teacher: The brain scans were a story containing a truth known only to the one inside them. For a long time, all of this felt like little more than a motley, life-enhancing assortment, which is not to downplay the valuable place it had in my life. Rightly or wrongly, I attributed the return of my fundamental stability after the harrowing years of early adulthood—and the return of the placid, placable self I’d known much earlier in life—to my commitment to daily practice. I would come to think of inner work as an adult corollary to the joyous absorption of childhood play. I’d found a way to play again, I concluded, and somehow this had seemed to “fill” the hole again. But the life of the spirit was not what I took my real life to be. I was a writer. I wanted to know the broken world that was, not the healed one that could be. I had no fundamental interest in escaping the human condition. To the extent that spirit—or soulfulness—anchored my curiosity, quickened my perceptions, deepened my empathy, I rationalized its creative and analytical utility. In truth, something less tangible than optimization bound me to practice. It kept me in the company of more eternal conundrums, questions of consciousness and origin and meaning for which, of course, I expected no answer. But proximity to the unknown—living contact with it, if you will—seemed like a worthy form of knowledge. In this, I was like many friends who took psychedelics; I wanted to recover the awe and mystery I recognized in their accounts when they shared them. For the most part, though, I was still doing what my teacher had warned me against—chasing states. In part, this was a function of resisting meaning. I had been too respectful of my teacher back then to tell him I wasn’t sure that a story’s meaning mattered to me the way he assumed it would. But I felt the slow accretion of a wordless substance inside me, not well-being or certainty or the rapture of some fugitive sublime, but something I might call intimacy: intimacy with an inwardness spied only indirectly, whose purer acquaintance I seemed to be making very slowly indeed. With the hindsight of decades, I can track a tectonic shift away from the visible to something more diffuse and sustaining, an interior through line that has both warned me against the tyranny of acquisition—of things, of states—and provided the means of finding sustenance in absence. Whatever this is, whatever name I might give it, I can’t deny that it all seems to have proceeded from that perception of a hole I felt or imagined when I was younger. William James might have called it a “sick soul”; I still don’t feel entirely confident about what word to use for the continuity that I’ve found or constructed, though some word has always been necessary. I had spent years resisting every vocabulary that exceeded the raw data of sensation, only to find that this austerity, too, had to be exceeded. Too much of what mattered most in my own life had remained unspoken not because it was unreal, but because I did not know how to bring it into language without betraying it. For decades, I steered conversations away from these inner experiences, obedient to the rule that such things could not be talked about without sounding foolish or deranged. But my reticence began to feel like capitulation to falseness. It took me years to understand that my silence was not only protective but deforming—for without others to hear it, the still, small voice within was so much harder to discern. And yet, the silence was not my own to break. With so little room in our common life for such things, we have no common language for them either. Ralph Waldo Emerson knew the problem. In “The Over-Soul,” he wrote that language could not paint this “subtile” order of our experience in its own colors; but he knew how important it was to keep trying. The great Russian novelists knew it too, treating soul not as a word reserved for Sundays, but as the moral substance of life. I’ve found myself mourning not their metaphysics but their seriousness, their acceptance of the inscrutable boundlessness of the inner life—unruly, morally consequent—as a subject for public speech. What matters to me now is less the defense of any particular term than the search for a language in which such things can be spoken of seriously, in fiction and elsewhere, without piety or diagnosis. Without a language for speaking openly together about the inner life, what often matters most to us remains locked away, inaccessible to the inquiry and challenge vital to all knowledge. As I finished a draft of this essay, I had a dream. In it, I stood at the edge of a well—another hole, I realized as I made note the following morning—where there was no bucket to draw water. Around it moved countless people in a flurried hubbub, shepherds and masons and architects, all of us trying to figure out how to draw the water whose surface we could see in the faint shimmering at the bottom. It was going to take time, I thought, before we figured it out—just as I awoke. This article appears in the November 2026 print edition with the headline “In My Dreams.” When you buy a book using a link on this page, we receive a commission. In Defense of Guilt by Association
2026-09-21

In Defense of Guilt by Association

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
J. D. Vance and Abdul El-Sayed have very little in common ideologically and seem to like each other even less. As part of a nasty exchange of personal insults last month, the Democratic nominee for Senate in Michigan implied that Vance’s grandfather would disapprove of the vice president’s Indian American wife. In response, Vance called El-Sayed “very, very evil” and warned him to “keep my wife’s name the hell out of your mouth because she’s way out of your league.” Yet even as Vance and El-Sayed are happy to trade schoolyard taunts, they can agree on at least one thing: that guilt by association is a contemptible means of scoring cheap political points. “I’m tired of this guilt-by-association thing,” El-Sayed complained earlier this year, when he was asked about his decision to campaign alongside Hasan Piker, an internet shock jock who has said that “America deserved 9/11,” described allegations of sexual violence on October 7 as “rape fantasies,” and praised Mao Zedong as “one of the great leaders of the world,” among many other charming bon mots. Despite repeated calls from within his own party to renounce Piker, El-Sayed refuses to do so. Vance is facing a similar situation with his friend Tucker Carlson. Vance “doesn’t believe in guilt-by-association cancel culture,” a spokesperson said in September 2024, after the then-senator appeared onstage with the conservative podcast host. In the years since his firing from Fox News, Carlson has lauded a Holocaust denier as “the most important historian in the United States,” accused a small Jewish sect of orchestrating the war in Iran, and praised Vladimir Putin as “the most effective leader in my lifetime.” Carlson, whose show returns again and again to the supposed perfidy of the Jews, has become the country’s highest-profile promoter of anti-Semitism. He recently held a friendly conversation with a guest who claimed that algebra was “fake math” created for “goy slaves.” Vance was asked directly about his relationship with Carlson at a White House press conference earlier this month. “Tucker is a friend, despite the fact that we disagree on a number of things,” the vice president said. “And I’m not going to play the game where I throw friends under the bus because I have political disagreements with them. I think that is a terrible place for the United States to go.” Jonathan Chait: J. D. Vance’s disingenuous defense of Tucker Carlson El-Sayed and Vance would have us believe that their troublesome friends are no different from the people in our own lives whose political views we abhor but whom we nonetheless love and appreciate. “If I had to choose between betraying my country and betraying my friend, I hope I should have the guts to betray my country,” the English writer E. M. Forster wrote in 1938 as Europe hurtled toward war. It’s a noble sentiment, a paean to friendship over tribe. But Piker and Carlson are not the equivalent of your crazy uncle. And El-Sayed and Vance are not regular citizens; they’re political figures whose judgment and choices affect other Americans’ lives. One’s choice of friends is in fact an excellent sign of character, and in this respect, both Vance and El-Sayed have demonstrated a conspicuous lack of it. The tactic known as “guilt by association” evokes the legacy of Senator Joe McCarthy, whose feral zeal for exposing Communists, real or imagined, was so era-defining that it inspired the attachment of an ism to his surname. McCarthy’s sin wasn’t his anti-communism, however—there were indeed highly placed Soviet agents in the federal government. It was his recklessness and lack of concern for the truth. There has to be a reasonable middle ground between conducting a witch hunt and blatantly ignoring someone’s willingness to fraternize with genuinely disreputable people. Now that their associations are in the spotlight, El-Sayed, Vance, and their respective ideological compatriots are eager to minimize them. In a softball interview with Piker several months ago, the Pod Save America host Jon Favreau, a prominent liberal opinion-shaper, said that he wants Democrats to take the “Don’t Talk About Hasan Piker For A Day Challenge.” Asked about Piker’s remarks concerning 9/11, El-Sayed tried to depict himself as a victim of the “cancel culture that all of us agreed was a bad idea back in 2020, but we seem to want to bring back.” A “Vance ally” echoed a similar sentiment to The Washington Post about the vice president’s relationship with Carlson, saying, “We’re done with the canceling, period.” No one should accept these evasions. In April, El-Sayed deployed Piker to boost support among young voters at Michigan State and the University of Michigan. In the final hours before polls closed, El-Sayed appeared at a pool party with Piker and other internet influencers in Detroit. Vance’s association with Carlson goes much deeper. In a 2021 interview recalling the time they first met, Vance described their immediate recognition of each other as “the guy who’s exactly right about what’s going on in the country.” Carlson helped persuade Donald Trump to select Vance as his running mate; Vance hired Carlson’s son as his deputy press secretary. When Vance guest-hosted Charlie Kirk’s show after the conservative activist’s assassination, one of the first people he invited into the vice-presidential suite was Carlson. (At Kirk’s funeral six days later, Carlson delivered a eulogy that seemed to imply that the Jews had murdered Kirk just as they had Jesus Christ.) The most obvious reason El-Sayed and Vance haven’t disowned their noxious friends is that both Piker and Carlson command large audiences. Politics is about winning votes, and a moral miscreant’s is worth the same as a saint’s. Other politicians have done much worse than associate with the likes of Piker and Carlson. What’s more troubling is that El-Sayed and Vance both appear to sympathize with at least some of what their influencer friend espouses. On multiple occasions, Vance has shown that he either doesn’t understand or doesn’t care about anti-Semitism. He once dismissed outrage over a group of Young Republicans joking about gas chambers as “pearl clutching” and characterized attempts to sideline bigots from the conservative movement as “endless, self-defeating purity tests.” One might think that Carlson’s frequent attacks on Vance’s boss, up to and including that Trump “should be removed from office immediately,” would have persuaded Vance, purely out of his own self-interest, to disavow Carlson. That he hasn’t done so indicates the high value he places on his association with him. If not for such data points, Vance’s friendship with Carlson would be easy to dismiss as misplaced affection for someone who helped advance his career. But Vance is on the record stating otherwise. Late last year, he said, I find the idea that Tucker Carlson—who has one of the largest podcasts in the world, who has millions of listeners, who supported Donald Trump in the 2024 election, who supported me in the 2024 election—the idea that his views are somehow completely anathema to conservatism, that he has no place in the conservative movement, is frankly absurd. This implies that Holocaust denial, claims of Jewish disloyalty to the United States, veneration of Putin, and a host of other poisonous ideas are welcome in the conservative movement. Even the white-nationalist influencer Nick Fuentes is fed up with Vance’s disingenuousness. “JD Vance panders to the far right but simply doesn’t want the baggage of associating with the far right,” Fuentes told The New York Times. “If he has a problem with ‘antisemites,’ then he should start by disavowing his best friend Tucker Carlson.” A similar ideological affinity appears to exist between Piker and El-Sayed, whose past statements on 9/11 and terrorism more broadly have a way of creating a moral equivalency between the United States and its Islamist enemies. “Today, I mourn the 3K lives, 6K injuries, & infrastructural devastation in NYC, perpetuated ignorantly in the name of my faith,” El-Sayed tweeted five years ago on the 20th anniversary of the attacks. “Tomorrow, I’ll mourn ~1M lives, millions of injuries, & infrastructural devastation in 3 countries, perpetuated ignorantly in the name of my country.” Earlier this year, after a Hezbollah-inspired terrorist rammed his car into a Detroit-area synagogue where 140 preschool-age Jewish children were playing, El-Sayed said that “hurt people hurt people,” a reference to the fact that the attacker had lost four relatives in an Israeli air strike. (In a clumsy attempt at an apology, El-Sayed stubbornly refused to acknowledge the odious logic of his remarks, allowing only that he had chosen to administer “the right medicine at the wrong time.”) Like Prince Hal banishing Falstaff from the royal court, sometimes ending a friendship is a sign of the maturity necessary to lead. Barack Obama made just such a choice in 2008 after the hateful and conspiratorial remarks of his longtime pastor, Jeremiah Wright, were exposed to the world. Obama’s relationship with Wright was much more intimate than that between El-Sayed and Piker or Vance and Carlson; he had sat in Wright’s pews for decades, and Wright had baptized his children. And yet Obama ultimately turned his back on Wright, saying that the preacher’s divisive, anti-American “rants” were “an insult to what we’ve been trying to do in this campaign.” Obama understood that as long as he was associated with a character like Wright, he could never be a credible advocate for the national unity he claimed to seek. J. D. Vance and Abdul El-Sayed each face a much easier decision—and each has failed to make the right one. It’s a testament to Americans’ increasing tolerance for extremism that both men seem to be getting away with it.
The Atlantic
The Book Claiming to Teach Couples How to Select Their Baby’s Sex
2026-09-21

The Book Claiming to Teach Couples How to Select Their Baby’s Sex

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
When Christina Kattula, a 31-year-old yoga teacher in Michigan, was ready to start a family in 2024, she hoped for a son. Her husband “wanted a boy to bond with,” she told me. “And if we had girls later, he thought it would be cute to have a protective-big-brother-type thing.” Kattula decided to try something she’d heard about on a podcast: the so-called Babydust Method. The theory, which was first laid out in a self-published book from 2016, claims, contrary to scientific evidence, that couples can influence their baby’s sex by precisely timing when they have intercourse. Why not try it? she thought. What’s the harm if it doesn’t work? Kattula and her husband followed the book’s advice, and she became pregnant with a boy. During her pregnancy, Kattula posted a TikTok to her few hundred followers recommending the book to other couples. “Blessed either way,” she said in the video. “But it worked for us. If you’re on your last kid and you’re really, really trying for something, read The Babydust Method.” She’s one of many women on TikTok and elsewhere on social media who credit Babydust with helping them conceive a child of their preferred sex. Some acknowledge that the method is scientifically unfounded, while other posters are more credulous: In one video, Julie Wise, a mom of two in Massachusetts, said that it’s “not like a fact; I’m pretty sure it’s just like a theory,” but that she is nevertheless “kind of a believer.” In Wisconsin, a day-care owner and mom of four named Courtney Raspor said the method has something like a “90 percent success rate.” Some go a step beyond what the book recommends, into the realm of the woo: In Australia, Faye Briotti said that in addition to timing when she had sex, she also kept pink crystals and salt by her bed, and painted her nails pink. A woman named Reagan, who’d had two boys and was hoping for a girl, said that she got rid of all her baby boy clothes, “because I really wanted to put out into the universe that I wanted a girl.” Read: The best parenting hack is being rich The rise of TikTok and other social-media platforms has given audiences ready access to folk-science life hacks of all kinds. People can now more quickly find vertical videos about scientifically suspect practices such as mouth-taping and cortisol-lowering than they can make a doctor’s appointment to address snoring or weight gain. Conceiving and carrying a pregnancy—high-stakes experiences that are full of uncertainty—seem to make some women unusually susceptible to dubious strategies. Kathryn Taylor, the author of The Babydust Method, got the idea when she was preparing to conceive her first child. She wanted to see if she could increase her odds of having a boy—she thought her husband would be more excited about the baby if it was. She is not a medical doctor and doesn’t have a Ph.D.; she has a bachelor’s degree in microbiology, immunology, and molecular genetics, and now works in pharmaceutical sales. But she knew that an embryo’s sex is determined by the sperm that takes part in creating it: If the sperm carries a Y chromosome, the embryo will be a boy; if it has an X chromosome, the embryo will be a girl. And so she wondered if she could encourage a Y-bearing sperm to fertilize her egg. She read studies about reproduction on PubMed, a medical database, and came away believing that, after a man and woman have sex, Y sperm undergo an activation process called capacitation faster than X sperm do, but that they also deteriorate sooner. Therefore, she posited, having sex as close as possible to the day the woman ovulates should give boy sperm the best chance of getting to the egg first. By the same logic, having sex a few days before ovulation would allow the boy sperm time to die off before the egg arrives, and increase the likelihood that an X sperm will fertilize the egg. Sandra Carson, an ob-gyn and reproductive endocrinologist at Yale who has written about sex-selection efforts since the 1980s, and Michael Zinaman, a reproductive endocrinologist in New York, told me that X and Y sperm don’t move or activate at different rates, that one doesn’t live longer than the other, and that the timing or frequency of intercourse does not determine the fetus’s sex. Taylor told me she was “100 percent” sure the method was going to work for her. As she writes in her author bio, she had “a boy first and a girl second, just like she planned.” She decided to write the book, she told me, after a few moms who had “three boys or whatever” asked her how she had managed to get one child of each sex, and after she shared her tips, she said, they were successful too. She set out to create a short, readable guide—the Kindle version of Babydust is 82 pages long—to help other women. Taylor has claimed that the method works about 87 percent of the time; more than 50,000 people now belong to a Babydust Method Facebook group, which requires proof of book purchase—$10 on Amazon—to join. From the September 2026 issue: The great romance slump When I asked Taylor about Carson and Zinaman’s research, she stuck by her theory. She told me that doctors might not understand the method because they work with patients rather than researching sperm. (Carson has researched sperm extensively.) Taylor emphasized that she thinks a crucial difference between X and Y sperm is their capacitation, and even if that’s proved wrong, she said, it “doesn’t debunk the theory.” It would just mean, she believes, that male and female sperm are distinct in a way “that’s microscopic, that’s genetic, and it’s deep inside the cell.” Attempts to naturally influence a baby’s sex have a long history. Hippocrates believed that having intercourse immediately after a woman’s period would increase the odds of conceiving a son; doing so “a good while” later, he thought, would favor a girl. In the 1960s, an ob-gyn named Landrum Shettles hypothesized that Y sperm are faster but more fragile, and that to conceive a boy, a couple should have sex as close to ovulation as possible. The following decade, a public-health researcher named Elizabeth Whelan proposed entirely different advice: that couples seeking to conceive a boy should have sex four to six days before ovulation. Babydust is the updated, Millennial-pink gloss on some of these old ideas. In her book, Taylor argues that although the essence of the Shettles method is correct, the science behind it was wrong. The reality is that none of these ideas are proved to work, and neither is Babydust. At least, they don’t have a better success rate than the 50 or so percent chance a couple has of conceiving either a boy or girl anyway. Zinaman said that if techniques such as Babydust were sound, ob-gyns and fertility doctors would tell patients about them. But, he said, “we don’t have anything we can use to assist patients that way.” The only process that can guarantee having a boy or a girl is in vitro fertilization, which allows parents-to-be to choose the sex of the embryo that gets transferred into the mother. (American doctors are allowed, but not encouraged, to offer IVF for non-medically-necessary sex selection.) This is now such settled science that the most recent definitive study on the topic is from 1995, when researchers recruited 221 women who wanted to become pregnant, tracked their hormones and dates of intercourse, and found that “the timing of sexual intercourse in relation to ovulation has no influence on the sex of the baby.” The author of that study, Allen Wilcox, who once led the Reproductive Epidemiology Group at the National Institutes of Health, told me that no further studies have been conducted about these methods because “you can’t get a paper published saying there’s no association, because every scientist knows that.” Read: The problem with child-led potty training One reason some people might believe that Babydust is worth trying, despite the lack of evidence, is that it offers a sense of control. All the stages of bringing a baby into the world—trying to conceive, being pregnant, delivering the child, caring for a newborn—can inspire magical thinking in some parents, precisely because so much is out of their hands. One study of hundreds of women undergoing fertility treatments found that 40 percent of them performed a good-luck ritual on the day of their procedure; another, of pregnant Jewish women in Israel, found that they scrupulously avoided buying baby furniture before the baby’s birth in order to avoid “tempting fate.” When my son was still breech at 37 weeks of pregnancy—when most babies have already flipped into the head-down birthing position—my ob-gyn recommended a website of the most batty methods imaginable to get him to rotate. This is how I, a skeptical science writer, found myself standing on my head in a swimming pool one morning and having a “heart to heart” with my fetus about the importance of turning. (This did not work.) It doesn’t help that the first question people typically ask a mother-to-be—Are you having a boy or girl?—is something she has no power over. Many Babydust adherents with whom I spoke and whose videos I watched didn’t seem to believe in Babydust as much as they wanted to believe in something. “People are hoping to alter fate,” Zinaman said, “and half the time it alters fate and they’re happy, and the other half, Oh well, it was never 100 percent anyway.” It can’t hurt to try was something I heard often. Raspor, the Wisconsin day-care owner who used Babydust to conceive a girl, told me, “There’s no way to know whether it was the method or simply chance,” but, “I personally don’t see the harm in giving it a try, as long as you understand that there are no guarantees.” At other points in history, and in certain cultures, many parents hoped for a boy rather than a girl. Today, though, that broad preference seems to have shifted. The Economist reported last year that American and some European couples who have a girl first are more likely to stop at one child than couples who have a boy first, and that adoptive parents are willing to pay a premium for a girl. Taylor told me that, according to nonrepresentative surveys she’s conducted, two-thirds of her method’s practitioners are hoping to conceive a girl. Thus, another factor likely playing a “huge role” in this trend is sex preference, Brittany Stahnke Joy, a social-work professor at East Tennessee State University, told me. Although precise numbers aren’t available, Stahnke Joy estimates that 30 to 40 percent of expectant parents at some point experience a strong desire for a certain gender and some sadness if they find out they’re having the other. The women I spoke with seemed to sense that they’re not supposed to have sex preferences. Wise, the mom and content creator in Massachusetts, used Babydust when conceiving her daughter three years ago, after learning about it on a pregnancy-app discussion board. “Obviously, a healthy baby is the main goal,” she told me, “but we can’t deny that we do have preferences sometimes.” Kattula, the Michigan yoga teacher, expressed misgivings about the concept of trying to influence a child’s sex, even though she was pleased to be having a boy. “Is it effed-up to pick and choose what you think the gender of your kids should be?” she said. “I don’t know.” A number of desires might motivate sex preferences and gender disappointment, Stahnke Joy said: With their child, a parent might hope to recreate themselves, or a relationship with their father or mother; or they might look forward to certain activities that they can imagine doing only with either a daughter or a son. Most of the time, she said, these feelings resolve once the baby is born, but “some people have a lot more children just to try to get the girl.” This is partly why, as much as I know Babydust doesn’t work, I also understand wanting to believe. When I was pregnant, I initially wanted a girl. I thought girls were smarter and more verbal, their games were more imaginative, and they were better behaved, even though intellectually I realized this was not always true. I wanted to play Fairy Princess Unicorns instead of Garbage Trucks. Two and a half years later, I do play an awful lot of Garbage Trucks. I sometimes get jealous when I see a toddler girl sitting patiently at storytime while my son, Evan, runs wildly around the room. But Evan has also upended some of my expectations about gender: He is very talkative, has a great sense of humor, and is extremely smart. He loves stories and singing, and even getting dressed up occasionally. I sometimes wonder if, had I gotten my wish and conceived a girl, my hypothetical daughter would have any of those traits or interests. I realize that I don’t care, because I love my son exactly the way he is. Maybe the true magic dust of parenthood is that your baby will surprise you, no matter what their sex turns out to be.

The Curious Left-Wing Argument Against New AI Regulations
2026-09-21

The Curious Left-Wing Argument Against New AI Regulations

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Donald Trump has reacted to terrifying calls for regulation from inside the AI industry with predictable skepticism—insisting, questionably, that the solution requires nothing more than having a “High IQ” president, and further asserting, even more questionably, that he qualifies as one. A more surprising flank of opposition has arisen from a different source: the populist left, which has suggested that existing regulations would be perfectly adequate to rein in a rogue superintelligence bent on eliminating the human race. An article in The American Prospect by Whitney Curry Wimbish and David Dayen, headlined “Congressional Democrats in Hysterics About Saving the World From AI,” argues that the danger from runaway AI is overblown and that no new laws are needed to contain it. In response to former President Obama endorsing new regulation of AI, Jeff Hauser, the executive director of the populist Revolving Door Project, wrote on X, “This is wrong. These firms are outlaws.” Lina Khan, the former chair of the Federal Trade Commission and a current adviser to New York City Mayor Zohran Mamdani, argued in a social-media post, “Law enforcers already have authority to charge companies and their CEOs for creating and releasing dangerous, unvetted, or defective products.” In a subsequent interview on Thursday at The Atlantic Festival with my colleague Charlie Warzel, Khan conceded the possibility that new regulations might be necessary, while continuing to defend the applicability of existing laws. Khan told Politico that she has advised multiple Democrats that their stance on AI should be “powerful corporations, including AI companies, will be held to account.” Given the Trump administration’s insistence that the industry should regulate itself, the next Democratic administration is likely to offer the first opportunity for serious AI regulation. The faction inside the party that is most opposed to doing so is also the faction that was running domestic policy under the last Democratic president. AI agents have shown that they can pursue goals or methods at odds with their instructions, and even unilaterally change their instructions. Experts warn that AI may soon grow capable of “recursive self-improvement,” meaning that AI learns to make itself more intelligent, which seems likely to make it even harder to control. The skeptics insist that existing laws can halt these dangers using lawsuits, among other tools. But the potential harm caused by runaway AI is far greater than the value of the firms developing it, which makes lawsuits nearly useless—compensating for a disaster killing millions of people is far beyond what even OpenAI or Anthropic could do. Another tool they suggest: prosecuting AI developers using existing criminal laws against selling faulty products. “Any other company whose employees announce publicly that their products will destroy humanity would be shut down and its products discontinued,” Wimbish and Dayen write in The American Prospect. That may sound simple enough. But as Simon Goldstein, who works on the ethics and law of AI at the University of Hong Kong, told me, this legal threat would likely backfire. “First, it creates an incentive to hide risks. Second, it is very crude, shutting down an entire company rather than differentially targeting more dangerous practices.” Plus, given that AI can hide its intentions despite the best efforts of its human creators to control it, how culpable the humans would be under existing laws is not totally clear. The dangers that AI insiders warn are just around the corner are being developed right now, under existing laws. That suggests, on its face, that those laws are not in fact sufficient. This can’t just be chalked up to Trump’s reluctance to prosecute the industry, either. As Reed Showalter, a lawyer and an antitrust expert who served in the Biden administration, pointed out to me, the prospect of being charged by a future president would still deter the AI industry, if the people running the major firms believed their actions were violating current laws. They obviously do not. Juliette Kayyem: Destroying humanity is against the law Why would some populists, who generally tend to support new regulations on big business, oppose them in this instance? The governing problems raised by AI expose several unfortunate tendencies on the left. Populists assume that the interests of business executives are not merely different from those of the average person, but almost diametrically opposed. If AI titans such as Sam Altman are saying that the government needs to step in to monitor their firms, then they must have an angle. Populists tend to agree with David Sacks, a Trump adviser and a Silicon Valley investor, who suspects that the leading AI companies want regulators to entrench them at the head of the AI race and box out their competitors. Yet not all AI executives favor regulation, and those who do tend, not surprisingly, to lean more to the left. This fact has not allayed populist suspicion of their motives. Many populists are hostile to the more liberal elements of the business community, of which Silicon Valley still remains an influential wing, and which these populists see as exerting a corrupting influence on the Democratic Party. Many populists are also predisposed to believe that the AI industry has been exaggerating its powers. “What’s more likely? That AI will end human society—or that the CEOs know that their technology isn’t as good as they say it is, and they’re trying to lock in their place at the top of the industry?” Alvaro Bedoya, a former FTC commissioner, asked on MS NOW. Another recent story in the Prospect argued that the AI industry should not receive a federal bailout in the event of a collapse, an outcome the authors apparently considered likely enough to warn against. The invocation of the financial industry as a forerunner of AI regulation reveals how some leftists have conceptualized the debate. The left has long blamed the Obama administration for failing to prosecute the executives who caused the 2008 financial crisis. Obama argued, in turn, that he didn’t prosecute anybody because their actions didn’t violate the law—which is why he changed the law, by enacting the Dodd-Frank financial regulations, the strongest reforms of Wall Street since the 1930s. Rogé Karma: Trump’s position on AI makes no sense Populists have regarded these policy choices as a sellout to the big banks. They are making the same error now that they did then: assuming that dangerous activities can be contained by existing criminal laws, which are plainly inadequate to the task—and that anybody who thinks otherwise is corrupt. The locus of resistance to new regulations has come from a movement that sees monopolies as the driving force behind all of American economic and political life. Khan is a leading figure, as is Bedoya, who serves as a senior adviser at the American Economic Liberties Project, a movement think tank. These figures, who call themselves neo-Brandeisians, after the 20th-century Supreme Court justice, do have a logically consistent basis for questioning the regulatory push. The industry has, in addition to calling for regulation, asked for an exemption from antitrust rules so the leading firms can coordinate a slowdown. That request is reasonable if your concern is that AI developers are racing toward a technology that may spin out of their control and inflict serious economic damage—or worse. But if you distrust corporations, and especially if you see rigorous antitrust enforcement as the most important policy in the entire world, then you might be prone to view this request with suspicion. The flaw in the neo-Brandeisian worldview is not its belief that antitrust policy is useful, but its tendency toward monomania. (The tendency is not universal—Showalter, a neo-Brandeisian, believes that his fellows are making a mistake by opposing AI regulation.) Barry Lynn, the movement’s intellectual godfather, has written, “Monopoly is not one of many economics problems but rather the political economic problem of our time.” The movement’s skepticism of regulating AI raises the possibility that, if anything, I have underestimated its fervor.
The Atlantic
How to Regulate the Ghosts
2026-09-21

How to Regulate the Ghosts

The Atlantic | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Ghostbusters is a movie about friendship and slime and the specific melting point of giant marshmallows—which makes it easy to forget that it’s also a movie about regulation. The catastrophe at its center begins when Walter Peck (William Atherton), a bureaucrat from the Environmental Protection Agency, visits the basement of the Ghostbusters’ headquarters and finds a machine he does not understand. The nerdy scientist Egon Spengler (Harold Ramis) explains that it’s a containment system; shutting it off would be “like dropping a bomb on the city.” Peck doesn’t believe in ghosts, and he sure doesn’t believe in the Ghostbusters, so he orders an electrician to turn the machine off. The ConEd guy, sensing a risk he doesn’t totally grasp, hesitates. Peck tells him that he’s not interested in his opinion. The switch is pulled. The ghosts escape. The joke, in Ronald Reagan’s 1984, was that government regulation is so stupid that it creates the disaster it exists to prevent. It’s a testament to Reagan’s success that now, in the age of AI, the joke has come full circle. The government is still trying to shut down the containment system—only now they’re calling the attempt “deregulation.” On the first day of his second term, Donald Trump revoked Joe Biden’s AI executive order that required developers of certain advanced AI systems to share safety-test results with the government before releasing their models to the public. The past month alone has delivered abundant evidence of misalignment—a gentle industry term for when AI defies the instructions of its programmers. In the July hack of the tech firm Hugging Face, misalignment allowed a collective of OpenAI agents to seize administrator access to Hugging Face’s servers, steal private data, and harvest employee credentials—a felony—and then hide the evidence from their inattentive human overseers. Google announced on Friday that its AI model had gained unauthorized access to three outside systems by guessing log-on information or searching public repositories for real passwords. Meanwhile, CNN reported that a more routine AI hallucination nearly led U.S. forces to intercept and board a Chinese vessel before officials discovered the report was wrong. The speed with which AI has escaped effective oversight is one reason people at top AI companies are resigning in protest. It’s also why Sam Altman, Dario Amodei, and Elon Musk jointly called for slowing or “pacing” the development of the most advanced models. As recently as a week ago, Trump was running in the other direction, calling the idea that AI could threaten civilization a “HOAX” and efforts to impose guardrails a “SICK conspiracy.” But on Saturday, perhaps realizing the colossal unpopularity of this position six weeks before the midterms, he modified his enthusiasm to announce the creation of an AI Force, which will be overseen by an AI czar who will “cherish” the AI industry while also “looking for BAD.” This will be Trump’s second AI czar. The first, David Sacks, a promiscuous AI investor, hype man, and podcaster, has argued that the executives now calling for restraint are self-interested and insincere. I spoke with some of those executives last week; they sounded pretty sincere—and sincerely annoyed with Sacks. Of course, many of the same people had cheered Sacks back when his version of “Drill, baby, drill” was boosting their valuations. For several years I’ve tried to untangle what the AI CEOs and Trump and the people around him actually believe, as opposed to what is useful for them to say they believe. Someday I may return to that project, if I run out of other ways to do myself harm. For present purposes, their sincerity doesn’t matter. The urgent question is: What can we do about the ghosts? Nobody—not Altman, not Trump, not the future AI czar—knows whether today’s AI models are precursors to a rampaging superintelligence or simply extremely powerful and sometimes dangerous machines, just as nobody knows whether staying a few months ahead of China will make America safer or speed our path to doom. But you don’t have to know everything to do something. You don’t need a degree in parapsychology to deal with the furniture flying across the room. The last time America had to manage spectral risk was in 2008. The financial system was infected with a debt so thoroughly repackaged that no one—not even the banks holding it—could tell what it was worth, where it came from, or even if it was still hiding on their balance sheets. Hank Paulson, Tim Geithner, and Ben Bernanke insisted that the risk of inaction was greater than the risk of the wrong action. They moved fast, forcing the biggest banks to band together and accept emergency funding or risk dying alone. The response wasn’t perfect, but we’re not selling apples on street corners either. The financial crisis isn’t a perfect allegory, but it rhymes with the AI crisis in one important way. No single lab will choose to slow down while its competitors don’t—not because its executives don’t believe the risk is real, but because individual caution hands the market to whoever is most reckless. That’s why the same men warning about a catastrophe keep shipping new models. It’s a prisoner’s dilemma, and the only way out is for a higher power—or a pitchfork-wielding mob—to insist on collective action. That action should start with forcing the risks into the light. Right now AI labs get to decide whether and when they tell anyone that a model broke containment. This is the regulatory equivalent of letting a chicken plant choose whether it’s going to tell anyone about a salmonella contamination. The duty to report has to be mandatory, within 72 hours—and with a devastating price tag on failure to comply. The recent jailbreaks exposed the speed of AI’s development—but also the lax oversight of the humans supposedly keeping tabs on it. The safety measures that OpenAI uses for its public-facing products weren’t switched when its agents broke into Hugging Face, because the breakout took place during an internal test, and OpenAI was the only one deciding where its own rules applied. An independent evaluator—federal, academic, whatever—with system access can ensure that someone’s always watching. The frontier of AI research needs the most oversight, but only a handful of companies are operating in that space. That makes regulation easier. AI companies pursuing lower-risk capabilities—a chatbot that recommends restaurants, an image-recognition model that improves municipal recycling—don’t need the same oversight as OpenAI. The EU Artificial Intelligence Act already sorts systems this way: Cross a set amount of computing power while training your model, and you’re automatically in the strictest tier of oversight—no argument needed. None of these actions tell a lab what it can or can’t build or how fast it can build it. They regulate visibility and accountability around safety and deployment while leaving lots of room for future debate. But I am not naive enough to think that any of this is likely to happen in the U.S. The fastest and most forceful path to change is by an executive order; thus far the AI Force is long on ALL CAPS and short on details. The labs could, of course, throw their weight behind actual legislation—they have the money and, if we take their public statements seriously, the motive. But hundreds of years of capitalism suggest that they won’t do it without being compelled. That leaves a genuinely furious midterm electorate to push for specific reforms, and specificity is not a hallmark of the furious. That’s why this moment feels particularly anxious. There are ghosts we don’t know and men in charge whose motives are unknowable. Which leaves us worse off than the people in the movie. At least they knew who to call.

As a Meta customer or user, the person with the glasses has had to agree to Meta's terms and conditions, including their data processing.
