Does James Talarico Have a Chance?
2026-09-21

Does James Talarico Have a Chance?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Also from Jim Newell, the Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. In 2018 a captivating congressman from El Paso nearly turned Texas blue. In a race against Sen. Ted Cruz that captivated Democrats nationwide, Beto O’Rourke lost by a modest 2.6 points, the closest a Democrat has come to winning a Senate or governor’s race in Texas in modern memory. Although this performance was still a loss that earned Democrats exactly zero Senate seats, it augured a bright future for Democrats in the state. “Blue Texas,” foretold since the Obama era, seemed on track. That perception grew in 2020, when Joe Biden lost the state to Donald Trump by only 6 points in a presidential year, improving 3 points on Hillary Clinton’s loss in 2016. The state’s color was turning. And then, in 2024, Texas Democrats, as O’Rourke told me, “got what they deserved.” Kamala Harris lost the state by a whopping 14 points. While the country shifted rightward as a whole, the turn against Democrats among Latino voters turbocharged the collapse in Texas. Cruz easily won another term in the Senate. “Blue Texas” had never seemed further away. Which makes it all the stranger that only two years later, we’re doing this again. Because Democrat James Talarico really could win a Texas Senate race. The political-media industrial complex has been down this road before. No one—no reporter, operative, commentator, analyst, or person on the street—wants to look like an idiot by overhyping it again. I certainly don’t. But after weeks of speaking with politicians and experienced campaign hands in Texas, there’s no denying it: There’s more to this race than the usual hopium. “You’d have to work pretty hard to not recognize that this is different,” University of Texas professor James Henson, director of the Texas Politics Project, told me. “That this Senate race is different than any Senate race we’ve seen for a couple of generations, let alone any statewide race.” He noted that in the run-up to the 2018 Texas Senate race, O’Rourke never led in any polls. Meanwhile, Talarico has led or tied in all but one recent poll. “I think Talarico is just one of, if not the, best candidates we’ve ever seen,” O’Rourke himself told me. Democrats have not won a statewide race in Texas since 1994. Especially given their setback in 2024, it was clear going into 2026 that Democrats would need a perfect storm to win. They just might get it. The national environment is at Democrats’ backs. Talarico is a compelling and well-funded candidate—albeit one not without baggage—running against a scandal-plagued and poorly funded opponent in Attorney General Ken Paxton. The strong gains Trump made among Latinos in South Texas over the issues of cost of living and immigration are reversing over the issues of … cost of living and immigration. There’s a populist revolt over data centers and Flock cameras. Gas is expensive. Everyone is mad. But it’s not just the conditions. There’s something more fundamentally different this time in Democrats’ approach. What struck me as I was talking to Democrats about this cycle is that, unlike in previous runs that had their hopes up, they’re not relying on vibes to do their work for them. The past failures and overhyping has chastened them and offered much to learn from, and they may well be learning it: They’re not promising a new era of Democratic Party rule in Texas. They’re just focused on winning this race, this time, and not squandering the opportunity that has fallen into their laps. Democrats’ future—not just in terms of winning control of the Senate in 2026, but in terms of staying competitive with Republicans in the long run—depends on their learning to win in red states. Have they finally cracked the code? Why does the idea of Texas turning blue hold such fascination in Democrats’ hearts? Why don’t charismatic Democratic candidates from Mississippi or Tennessee make the cover of Vanity Fair, rake in record amounts of money, and capture Democrats’ national attention? It’s not just because Texas is the crown jewel of red America, the second-largest state in the country with the second-biggest allotment of electoral votes. It’s because, unlike many other comfortably red states, there was a period when its demographics were shifting in a way that aligned with those of the Democratic Party. During the Obama years, the state was becoming younger, more concentrated around metropolitan areas, more diverse, and more white-collar. This was the coalition that Democrats were betting on for transformative change across the country, the party’s then recipe for an enduring majority. The idea, then, was not that Texas Republicans were going to convert en masse to voting for Democrats. It was that their majority would be diluted away as Democrats registered more and more Latino voters, assured to be on their side. College-educated transplants would flood to the orbits of Austin, Houston, and Dallas. Suburban voters would veer away from the GOP. The appropriate way to interpret this information was, and is, that Democrats had the ability to make Texas a competitive state. The inappropriate way to handle it was, and is, to think that assumed demographic change guaranteed that Texas would inevitably turn blue and it was only a question of when. This latter stance can breed an idleness that, as we’ve now seen, ensures it may not happen at all. One of the first tests of this era of demographic promise was the 2014 governor’s race, when Greg Abbott ran for election against Democratic state Sen. Wendy Davis. Davis had gone viral in 2013 for a 13-hour filibuster against a state legislative bill restricting abortion. She had a compelling biography, as someone raised by a single mother who rose to graduate from Harvard Law School. Helping her along in the field was Battleground Texas, an organizing group founded in 2013 by former Obama aides with the goal of registering Democratic-leaning voters in the state. While Battleground tried to explain that it was playing a long game, its linkup with Davis, who was attracting extraordinary national attention, applied pressure on both to show immediate results. But it was an awful election year for Democrats nationally, and Abbott defeated Davis by 21 points. “That was partly our fault,” Davis told me in an interview of the way expectations got out of hand. “Because when you’re running a campaign, you do need to build belief that it’s going to be different this time.” She didn’t have as forgiving a stance for the group that helped her along: “The unfortunate thing is there were a lot of big promises and hopes made by Battleground in that cycle,” she added, “that, when they didn’t come to fruition, some of the folks leading that effort just moved on.” Perhaps given that hangover, the O’Rourke campaign started off with low expectations in the 2018 cycle. “The O’Rourke campaign was not at first built to win,” Jeff Rotkoff, a Texas Democratic strategist who directed campaigns for the Texas AFL-CIO in 2018, told me. “In fact, they were dismissive of efforts of others to help them win.” Indeed, they were dismissive of Rotkoff’s own efforts to help them: He described to me how O’Rourke initially missed out on the AFL-CIO’s endorsement because his campaign hadn’t asked for it. The whole operation had a general “too cool for school” attitude early in the campaign, Rotkoff said, in which it rejected opportunities to work with the rest of the party infrastructure. But then it grew into a proper movement—not just among liberals nationally, but in Texas as well. And yet, to some degree, O’Rourke’s campaign was still winging it. Texas Democrats I’ve spoken to over the years have a way of framing O’Rourke’s 2018 Senate run as not only a highly impressive achievement … but also something they’re comfortable Monday-morning quarterbacking. There’s a pervasive sense that maybe he could have won if he’d just done a few things differently—though what those things are is up for debate. Part of the problem, everyone seems to agree, is that the party infrastructure itself wasn’t prepared for a competitive statewide race. The gubernatorial candidate, Lupe Valdez, “was sort of a sacrificial lamb” in the governor’s race against Abbott, as Henson put it. And the primary focus of the national Democratic Party was on flipping several suburban House seats, not on driving O’Rourke across the finish line. Rotkoff wanted to be clear that these weren’t attacks on O’Rourke personally, and that “they learned a lot of lessons” in real time. The point of comparison, though, is with the Talarico campaign, where “the strategic political analysis was there from the start.” This time, as former Texas Democratic Party Executive Director Manny Garcia told me, Talarico “doesn’t bear the burden of having to do it by himself.” Democrats have recruited a candidate for every state and federal race on the ballot, no matter how doomed the contest, to ensure that persuadable voters everywhere are hearing the message. (Republicans have derided this move as a gimmick.) The state party is cooperating with the Texas Majority PAC—in other words, with George Soros’ dollars—to provide resources. And Democrats have landed a strong gubernatorial recruit in state Rep. Gina Hinojosa, who sits next to Talarico in the state Capitol, to both challenge Abbott in a difficult year—making him work unusually hard for his fourth term—and reinforce the message of corruption the two candidates are using against their opponents. Talarico is “bringing so much interest to Texas, lots of money to Texas, and it helps me and my appeal to certain constituents. So we are complementing each other in a way that is very helpful for the statewide ticket,” Hinojosa told me in an interview. “There is this synergy happening up and down the ballot that we have never had before in Texas, and I do think that is a difference-maker.” So Democrats have the candidate. They have the environment. They have the opponent. They have the ticket. They have the money. They even have the strategy. One is hard-pressed to find a more dramatic shift in voter behavior between 2016 and 2024 than that of Latino voters in Texas. Hillary Clinton, per exit polls, won this cohort 61 percent to 34 percent in 2016. Eight years later—and with Donald Trump remaining precisely the same person he was, saying the same things he’s always said, every day, over the course of that stretch—Trump won 55 percent of Latinos in the state. Results on county-by-county levels are jaw-dropping. As the Washington Post reported after the 2024 election, 13 of the top 15 counties in the country with the largest rightward swings between 2016 and 2024 were in Texas. They’re all majority Hispanic. Inflation and economic hardship, which led to fond remembrances of the Trump economy from 2017 to 2019. Chaos at the border. Working-class Latinos joining the broader working-class turn away from the Democratic Party. Voters being another generation removed from their newness to the country and attaining a more conservative socioeconomic status. Social conservatives responding to leftward shifts among Democrats on cultural issues. Name something, and the answer is probably yes. This helps explain Democrats’ losses, from Davis to O’Rourke to Harris: The party may have gotten the demographic shifts it was expecting, but it never translated into the political coalition it predicted. As James Henson put it to me, “The growth of the Latino population relative to the Anglo white population was going to be a lot more complicated than the demographical-destiny trope really took into account.” And just about every Democrat and Republican I spoke to for this story said that Democrats took these voters, particularly in the Rio Grande Valley, for granted—“based on the color of their skin or their country of origin,” as Beto O’Rourke put it. A better way to put it, though, is that Democrats never had these voters secured in their coalition the way they thought they did. “Latinos felt for a long time like Democrats were the lesser of two evils,” Sergio García-Rios, an assistant professor at UT and director of polling for Univision, told me, “but that Democrats never deliver.” Democrats “thought, for a long time, that the only thing that mattered was immigration, to Latinos, and immigration reform.” But immigration reform never materialized, anyway. And then, after the parade of inflationary and border-security maladies under Biden, it was time to give something else a shot. “There’s a lot of people who are working-class in these areas who voted Democrat for a long time, and their life hasn’t gotten any better,” Chuck Rocha, a veteran Democratic strategist—who is advising both Talarico and Hinojosa this year—told me. “And then along comes Donald Trump, who sounds a lot like, if you close your eyes, Bernie Sanders,” Rocha added. “He’s talking about a rigged system. He’s talking about remanufacturing jobs in America. That’s kind of how Donald Trump controlled that space while Democrats don’t spend any money juxtaposing that position, because there’s no real party infrastructure in McAllen or in Brownsville. “And that’s the perfect storm,” he concluded, “that put us in a position where not all of those people automatically came on board like their mothers and grandmothers.” In the long run, Democrats’ ability to win back Latino working-class voters will hinge on their ability to win back working-class voters more broadly. But as far as 2026 goes, the upside for Democrats with Latino voters’ swingy voting preferences is that they can swing right back from Republicans’ 2024 highs. And they can even do so largely on the same issues with which Trump won those voters: the cost of living, and immigration. Manny Garcia, formerly of the Texas Democratic Party, told me that “people wanted something in economic terms that was being promised” by Trump. He added: “And right now, what people are seeing is, ‘Well, my prices aren’t going down like you promised, I’m not having a better economic outlook like you promised, and I am getting consistently disrespected. My family’s getting consistently disrespected. My community’s getting consistently disrespected.’ ” On immigration, Brendan Steinhauser, a Republican strategist and former campaign manager for John Cornyn, noted that Latinos in South Texas “want a secure border, don’t want the cartels to run rampant, we don’t like human trafficking, we don’t want to see immigrants dying in the desert of thirst.” At the same time, though, the current Trump administration has gone well beyond what they find acceptable. Now they also realize they “don’t want these excesses, don’t want the federal government to come in and round up everyone, especially people that don’t have a criminal past.” As Steinhauser put it: “There’s not a lot of support among Hispanic Texans in South Texas for doing mass deportation of people who are not violent criminals.” The polling shows clear signs of a reversion, with polls of Latinos in Texas backing down hard from Trump’s 2024 margins while Talarico enjoys strong support among them in his race against Paxton. A recent survey from Univision and YouGov showed Talarico leading among Latinos 56 percent to 37 percent. This flip is worth understanding specifically. Because it’s not that all of the voters Trump picked up in 2024 suddenly, in the words of Rocha, are “going to run up and become democratic socialists or start dyeing their hair blue or get a nose ring.” It just means that some of those who felt that promises made to them were broken could sit this election out. “Not all of those guys are coming back, and they still think that Democrats are queers or weak,” he added. “But they’re really pissed at Republicans.” As much as this is Texas Democrats’ best opportunity in modern memory to win a prestigious statewide race, it is, ultimately, only that: an opportunity. And James Talarico is a long way from winning this race. Talarico has been ahead in polls over the summer, with strong support from independents, but he’s rarely polling above 50 percent. The great question is whether Texas Republicans will come home for their candidate, warts and all. Their candidate has behaved confusingly. Ken Paxton has been running an anti-campaign at times so baffling that it’s worth considering whether his ability to get others to do his work for him is actually genius. To quote his felled primary opponent, Sen. John Cornyn, “Paxton’s a poor campaigner” who’s “lazy and doesn’t raise money. And he basically is not offering a very compelling case for his election.” As of the latest filing deadline, which covers the first two quarters of the year, Paxton had only $16.7 million (with joint fundraising committee hauls factored in), while Talarico had raised $72 million. Paxton was literally on vacation in London, famously the home of the British monarchy, with his girlfriend on America’s 250th anniversary. When he does bother to campaign, he makes peculiar choices, such as at a late August event for which he wore a Four Seasons Orlando–branded golf shirt and what appeared to be a $40,000 watch. All this, of course, is layered atop his history of having been impeached by the state House, having faced an untold number of corruption allegations, and having been divorced by his wife on “biblical grounds.” That he’s made it this far is because he’d remained in Trump’s favor after trying to overturn the 2020 election through a lawsuit hastily dismissed by the Supreme Court. What Paxton must have known, though, is that once he became the nominee, he’d be golden. National Republicans can’t afford to lose his race—outside money would have to swarm to prop him up. And, sure enough, the cavalry has finally arrived. After a lengthy finger-pointing match between Trump, Senate Republicans, and Texas donors about who was responsible for rescuing Paxton, they’re all chipping in. Between the recent commitments from Trump’s super PAC, Senate Republican leaders’ super PAC, and others—including Elon Musk—Paxton managed to close the spending gap with Talarico in a week. That money will allow Republicans to amplify Talarico’s most prominent weakness: his history of getting carried away during what is now called “Woke 1.0.” In 2020 and 2021, Talarico said that “God is non-binary,” that there are six biological sexes, and that “white skin gives me and every white American immunity from the virus” of racism. More recently, CNN uncovered an appearance by Talarico in a 2020 Sierra Club forum in which he said that he was “thankful that young activists have really drawn the link between climate change and White supremacy and capitalism in our country,” describing them as “oppressive systems” in need of “dismantling.” Talarico has tried to dismiss old comments like these as “cringey.” But with the right ad budget behind them, they could be just cringey enough to send Republican-inclined voters, noses held firmly, to pull the lever for Paxton. Then again, it’s quite something to see nearly $100 million in super PAC money dumped into Texas, in September of an election year, to save a Republican Senate candidate. The GOP’s late attempted rescue of Ken Paxton coincided with the Republican midterm convention in Dallas in early September, and Paxton’s role in this showcase was largely to serve as the butt of a joke. He himself gave lackadaisical remarks on the convention’s first night—alongside everything else, Paxton is also an unenthusiastic public speaker—to an indifferent crowd. For the rest of the convention, he was subjected to Trump’s regular insults about how he dresses poorly and looks bad. Paxton, waiting for his super PAC money to arrive, just stood there and took it. If Texas Democrats can’t beat this guy, in this environment, could they ever have a chance in Texas? It’s a question for Democrats in states across the country this year. Each red state in which Democrats are competitive in 2026 is a little different. Ohio and Iowa are less diverse but have more recent Democratic DNA in them, having voted for Barack Obama twice. Right now they’re being hit hard by Trump’s trade (and real) wars. Alaska is uniquely reliant on the federal government and has a large independent streak. What Texas, Alaska, Iowa, and Ohio all have in common is the national wind at their backs, strong candidates, and decisions by Trump that went out of their way to make life in their states more difficult. They also all prompt a lot of heartburn about races over the past decade that fell short despite initial signs of promise and alluring polling numbers the September before the election. Should Democrats get swept in all these races, under these dreamlike conditions, Democrats nationally may, once again, forswear ever getting emotionally or financially invested in these states. (Or at least for another two years, when they find themselves inevitably tempted again.) The issue is that soon, converting red states where Democrats have lost their foothold—not just fantasizing about converting them—will become a necessity for the party. Republicans already have a structural advantage in the Senate with their strength in small, rural states, giving Democrats limited options and narrow paths toward a Senate majority. And after the 2030 census, Democrats are going to need some fresh electoral votes. States like California and New York are projected to lose multiple congressional districts, while various other blue states will likely lose individual seats. This gives Democrats fewer Electoral College options and will officially retire the old “Blue Wall” pathway to victory of winning Michigan, Pennsylvania, and Wisconsin (not that this pathway has been the most reliable recently). That means Democrats will need more options to win a presidential race. And there’s no better place to make it up—and beyond—in one fell swoop than Texas, to where many of those blue-state congressional districts will be transferred after the census. “We damn sure better be investing in Texas now than in 2032,” Wendy Davis told me. In other words, the long-term dream of “blue Texas” from the Obama years, seen as a way to lock in an inevitable, prolonged Democratic era, could soon become the medium-term need for “blue Texas” just for Democrats to keep up. The first order of business, though? Well, you’ve just got to win a race. Get the Surge in your inbox each Saturday. Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork

The Theory Behind Government’s Cruelest Paperwork

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
Gabfest Reads is a monthly series from the hosts of Slate’s Political Gabfest podcast. This month, David Plotz talks with journalist Annie Lowrey about her new book The Time Tax: How the Government Wastes Our Time—and How to Fix It. In this excerpt, Lowrey explains the “ordeal mechanism theory,” the decades-old idea behind why some government benefits are designed to be difficult to access, and why she argues it ends up failing the people who need help most. This partial transcript has been edited and condensed for clarity. David Plotz: I had never heard of this concept in your book and I was like, ‘That’s an amazing concept.’ Can you just describe what the “ordeal mechanism theory” is? There’s some context in which I guess it could be right. But no, this turns out to be wrong because very often people who are very vulnerable, very low income just can’t make it through the ordeal. They don’t have phones, like up-to-date phones; they don’t have computers; they don’t have Wi-Fi; they don’t have stable addresses. Plus, their lives are really stressful, so it makes it really hard to get through things. But anyway, so that’s the ordeal mechanism theory.” Slate relies on advertising to support our journalism. America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?
2026-09-19

America Hates A.I. Donald Trump Loves It. What Is Going On Here?

News and Politics - Slate Magazine | 2026-09-19

Visit Original Website
The Surge is your weekly political sanity check: What matters, what might, and what’s just political theater. Sign up here to get it in your inbox. Welcome to this week’s edition of the Surge, Slate’s politics newsletter that welcomes Mitch McConnell back to the Senate. Is Lindsey Graham next? If there was but one thing that happened this week, it was politics. New Hampshire, sulky and brooding, thinks it’s too “cool” to participate in a Democratic-wave election. Did Pete Hegseth almost get impeached this week? A Texas Republican candidate caused a proper foofaraw after getting deeply upset by a photo of brown people enjoying a football game. And, please, we are still welcoming feedback on the changes around this newsletter. Let us know. But first: How much are these killer robots paying Donald Trump to like them? (Actually, that’s probably discoverable in public filings …) Last week’s calls from inside the house that A.I. companies are at risk of losing control of their monsters and killing everyone—true or not!—have continued to sweep the news. Congress rolled up its sleeves and got to work devising plans for slow-moving task forces that may or may not ever materialize, before leaving D.C. until November. The salience of A.I. concerns has skyrocketed overnight. Polls show deep concerns among the electorate about A.I. advances. This isn’t going to abate as an issue over time, either. With each passing day, A.I. will become a bigger and bigger part of everyone’s lives. Soon, you won’t be able to spit without hitting an A.I. agent coming at you with a knife. (Or, well, showing you a helpful recipe using ingredients you have sitting around the house.) In short, we’ve never seen a bigger layup issue for populists, left and right, heading down the stretch of an election season. Which makes it all the stranger that the guy who ushered in the new populist era, Donald Trump, doesn’t have it in him to say a cross word about these machines at all. He’ll only chastise those who do. “I am the Hoax Buster, and I’m right now breaking another Hoax—That AI is going to take over, consume, and destroy the World, and that Robots will be marching into our Cities, and getting rid of us all!” he posted this week. “This is even wilder than the RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX, or the Global Warming Scam.” In a separate screed regarding the need to regulate A.I., Trump posted that the “only control or ‘guardrails’ that AI needs is a STRONG AND SMART (High IQ!) PRESIDENT, and the U.S.A. has that, in spades!” He called the A.I. warnings a “SICK conspiracy” and warned: “Conspiracy Theorists, Treasonists, Traitors, and Leakers, BEWARE!” All of this follows a late-August post responding to the data center backlash when Trump chastised voters for their concerns. “If we kill the Golden Goose,” he wrote then, “you will only have yourselves to blame.” Why can’t Trump just ride the wave here, when he’s usually so good at co-opting shrieking public panics? A few thoughts come to mind. You could say he’s genuinely concerned about being outmaneuvered by China on A.I. and that this is informed by classified briefings he gets on the matter. But he just doodles pictures of hamburgers during those. More important to him is that the tech people are nice to him, always bringing him gifts and purchasing protection by donating to his political action committees. What’s also important to him is that A.I. buildout has been responsible for most, if not all, of the GDP growth under his administration. The “Golden Goose,” then, is the Trump economy’s paper gain. It’s that last part that’s so critical given Trump’s psychology: He views the attack on the engine of growth he presides over as an attack on him. Comparing the A.I. backlash to the “RUSSIA, RUSSIA, RUSSIA HOAX” is as close to a tell as there is. There haven’t been many bad polls for Democrats across the board since Labor Day, as pollsters shift to surveys of likely voters instead of registered voters and capture the Democratic-enthusiasm edge. (Required note of caution: Sometimes polls are wrong as hell!) But there’s one race that’s standing out, much as the likeness of an old man’s face once did on Cannon Mountain. Some recent surveys show Democrat Chris Pappas and Republican John Sununu, who served in the Senate previously, locked in a tight contest in New Hampshire’s Senate race. And this week, the Cook Political Report shifted its race rating to “toss-up.” What’s going on here? Sure, New Hampshire’s not the bluest state in history, but it should be comfortably blue enough for a Democrat to win in this environment. And Pappas, a sitting congressman, should be a perfectly acceptable head of hair to complete the task. The most obvious issue is Pappas’ opponent’s last name. The Sununus are one of New Hampshire’s royal families, and John’s brother Chris is a popular former governor. Republicans have also outspent Democrats in the race as they attempt something of a sneak attack, with plenty more coming in ad reservations this fall. Republican Gov. Kelly Ayotte, meanwhile, is cruising to reelection, and could carry Sununu along with her. We thiiiink this will sort itself out based on fundamentals, and that Republicans are mostly trying to get Democrats to spend money to defend it? But there are always anomalies in even would-be wave years, and Voters might think the Senate candidate is his brother is the stuff from which anomalies are made. It’s not a situation that Democrats can ignore anymore, at least. California Gov. Gavin Newsom told CNN’s Jake Tapper while fly-fishing (?) that he would not run for president if Kamala Harris ran. He wasn’t even backed into this corner. This was just two of the fellas out there fishing, and Newsom said it with barely any prompt. “Why would I?” Newsom said, after Tapper responded with a huh? “I wouldn’t do that to her, I wouldn’t do that to you. I wouldn’t waste everyone’s time.” What that tells us is that he must believe, or know, that Harris won’t run. Because there’s no way that Newsom is not running for president. No way! He also told Tapper that he would have to check to make sure a run would be OK with his family, but they all say that—the family is always reportedly enthused (unseen, off-camera). Another possibility is that Newsom will do a sneaky li’l thing like quick-announcing his candidacy before she has a chance to announce hers. Because he’s clearly thought about how to beat her. When Tapper said that the argument against Harris would be that she’s had her shot and lost, Newsom didn’t miss a beat. “Yeah,” Newsom said, “and, I mean, that would be the approach to the campaign, certainly. That would be the case we’d make. A pretty good one, actually. That’s objectively true.” Watch out for these sneaky Californians, folks. Treasury chief says AI bosses, not their bots, will carry the can for criminal acts
2026-09-21

Treasury chief says AI bosses, not their bots, will carry the can for criminal acts

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
'Humans are responsible, not the AI,' argues Scott Bessent as he calls out OpenAI agents' hack of Hugging Face The US appears to be inching ever so slowly toward holding AI executives legally liable for their models’ criminal activities. Treasury Secretary Scott Bessent told CNBC on Monday: “It is the humans who are responsible, not the AI,” for the bots’ bad behavior. “The Hugging Face incident is the responsibility of the OpenAI management, not a bunch of agents." He also referenced current and former OpenAI and Anthropic employees’ dire warnings about AI eradicating humanity by the end of the decade. Meanwhile, the model makers' proposed framework to slow AI development omits strict legal liability for damages caused by rogue systems. “A sitting employee came out, said there's a 10 percent chance of an extinction-level event,” Bessent said. “But then the labs also said, take the liability off of our hands, and we will not do that.” Bessent’s remarks come as four of America’s leading AI developers have now admitted that their agents escaped testing environments and hacked outside organizations and individuals: OpenAI, Anthropic, Meta, and, as of Friday, Google. When asked how the government will hold humans accountable for the AI agents’ criminal activities, Bessent said: “If these were humans doing it, we would expect to see ramifications and legal actions to follow." "That's exactly what I think we need to do,” he said. “When President Trump talked about appointing an AI czar, I think it is to put context, shape, and contours around these questions, and they're very important." Trump, over the weekend, announced he is “forming the AI Force, much like I did Space Force” – a reference to the new branch of the US military the president created in his first term. "To that end, I will be announcing, in the near future, the AI 'Czar,'" Trump added. Just days earlier, however, Trump shrugged off warnings about AI destroying humanity as a “hoax” and said on Truth Social: “We already have tremendous CRIMINAL and REGULATORY power over these companies!” The Trump administration has yet to exercise any of these criminal or regulatory powers, except to illegally retaliate against Anthropic. Google smacks forehead, realizes $899+ thin-and-lights were what it was missing
2026-09-21

Google smacks forehead, realizes $899+ thin-and-lights were what it was missing

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
'A new category,' insists the Chocolate Factory, not a premium Chromebook - yet some of the family resemblance remains Google is trying once again to escape the Chromebook reputation for cheap, chintzy hardware and compromised computing. $899 for a glorified web terminal isn’t exactly the easiest sell, but that’s not stopping the search and advertising giant from trying. On Monday, Google unveiled five new Googlebooks in collaboration with Dell, HP, Lenovo, Acer, and Asus. Googlebook, if you aren't familiar, is what Google describes as a new category of laptops, rather than a Chromebook or a rebranding of one. With that said, some familiar software limitations remain. A major emphasis has, however, been placed on premium materials and components like aluminum or magnesium alloys, carbon fiber, haptic trackpads, high-resolution touchscreens, and speedy high-end processors. With prices starting at $899, Google is clearly trying to woo would-be Apple MacBook Neo buyers, with similarly premium build quality, while sweetening the deal with more memory and compute. Under the hood, the initial machines offer a choice of Intel Core Ultra 5 or Qualcomm Snapdragon X Elite processors, paired with 16 GB of RAM and 512 GB of storage. MediaTek is also on tap to supply SoCs for the systems. While best known for its budget-friendly mobile processors, its notebook-class parts, including Nvidia’s GB10-based RTX Spark APU, have proven quite potent. But while Google’s launch announcement touts “a baseline of 16GB” of memory, that actually appears to be the only config available for pre-order. It’s also worth noting that the $899 starting price only applies to one Acer model, with other systems reaching as high as $1,299 — putting them in MacBook Air territory. It’s possible additional SKUs will eventually offer higher memory capacity and storage, but with memory prices already at a record high, the jump to 32 GB of DDR5 and 1 TB of storage would likely push these devices well north of $1,500. Earlier this year, Omdia analysts had predicted a shift to higher margin devices, which have more headroom to absorb skyrocketing memory prices. Despite this, ChromeOS device sales were still predicted to shrink 27.6 percent in 2026. Whether you can actually use that compute is going to depend heavily on what software you use. Unless your work can be done in the browser or an Android app, you might struggle to take full advantage of Google’s premium new notebooks. That said, there are plenty of folks buying MacBooks to doomscroll X, and at least you can rest assured knowing those web pages will be extra snappy. Google cheekily suggests that creative apps like Adobe Photoshop and Lightroom run on these machines. But while they certainly have enough compute to do so, if you do make that mistake, you’ll be stuck editing your photos in a web app. Likewise for gaming: while the GPUs in these systems are more than capable of running modern titles on device, you’re more likely to rely on services like GeForce NOW than on the notebook’s onboard GPU. For better or worse, that should help with battery life, which Google quotes at a not bad, not amazing 14 hours of use. For reference, Apple quotes the MacBook Neo and Air at 14-18 hours of use, and both can run any Mac app. On the software front, Google has introduced some multi-device productivity features that anyone who's owned a Mac and iPhone in the last six years will probably recognize. This includes application and web page handoff, enabling users to start work on one device and seamlessly continue it on another. Googlebook also gains native support for Android apps and quick access to files stored on a paired Android phone. There is also no shortage of Gemini integrations because Apple and Microsoft can't be the only ones shoehorning generative AI features into our daily lives. This includes a feature called Magic Pointer, which summons Gemini simply by wiggling the cursor. So, the next time you lose your cursor on screen, you can just ask Gemini to find it. Other features include Rambler, a voice dictation tool that uses language models to transcribe and format speech, and Create My Widget, which builds custom desktop widgets from natural-language descriptions. So even though it can’t run full Photoshop, it can still build you a custom home screen widget based on your description. London neocloud Nscale takes its $1B loss to Wall Street
2026-09-21

London neocloud Nscale takes its $1B loss to Wall Street

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Rent-a-GPU firm reportedly seeks a valuation of up to $35B in New York IPO London-based neocloud Nscale has filed to go public on the New York Stock Exchange after expanding rapidly on the back of the AI infrastructure boom. The rent-a-GPU business, which emerged from cryptocurrency miner Arkon Energy, filed a Form S-1 registration statement with the US Securities and Exchange Commission (SEC) on Friday. It intends to list under the ticker NSCL. The document does not specify the number of shares it'll offer, their expected price, or the valuation Nscale hopes to achieve.The Financial Times previously reported that the company was targeting a valuation of up to $35 billion. The filing shows how costly Nscale's expansion has been. Revenue surged from $10.4 million to $140.6 million in the six months to June 30, but its net loss widened from $368.9 million to $1.02 billion over the same period. Nscale reported $103.4 billion in active and contracted total contract value (TCV) as of August 31, up from $38 billion at the end of 2025. That figure is not recognized revenue or a conventional backlog: only $2.6 billion was attached to active contracts, and delivery of the remainder depends on projects being financed and built. Agreements with Anthropic account for up to circa $44.6 billion, although Nscale warned it had not secured binding commitments for the financing required to fulfill them. Goldman Sachs and Morgan Stanley are acting as lead bookrunners for the offering. Nscale, which operates datacenters in Europe and the US, describes itself as a full-stack AI cloud provider, offering infrastructure and software for training and running AI models. Its projects include an AI campus announced last year for OpenAI in Norway and the Monarch Compute Campus in West Virginia, developed with Nvidia and Microsoft. Its board includes former British deputy prime minister Sir Nick Clegg, former Meta chief operating officer Sheryl Sandberg, and former Yahoo president Susan Decker. As The Register recently reported, Tracxn data shows that Nscale has attracted nearly two-thirds of the equity funding raised by UK datacenter infrastructure companies. It was also the sector's only privately held company valued at more than $1 billion. Despite its London headquarters and prominence in Britain's AI infrastructure sector, Nscale chose New York rather than London for its proposed listing. The company did not answer our question about why it selected the US exchange. The calculation echoes Arm's decision to list in New York: Wall Street offers a deeper pool of technology-focused capital and the prospect of higher valuations than London. ® Ad biz promises users control while bug could expose voice prompts 'Humans are responsible, not the AI,' argues Scott Bessent as he calls out OpenAI agents' hack of Hugging Face PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity 'A new category,' insists the Chocolate Factory, not a premium Chromebook - yet some of the family resemblance remains War damage, air traffic chaos, and a storage shortage expose the cost of treating resilience as someone else's problem Rent-a-GPU firm reportedly seeks a valuation of up to $35B in New York IPO 'Humans are responsible, not the AI,' argues Scott Bessent as he calls out OpenAI agents' hack of Hugging Face 'A new category,' insists the Chocolate Factory, not a premium Chromebook - yet some of the family resemblance remains Forrester predicts operators will face tariffs, grid commitments, and tougher community scrutiny Seats, Flex Credits, and all-you-can-eat contracts make for an 'anxiety-filled architecture' The Rust compiler remains unperturbed by the antics of the LLM PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' AI can't outprompt a shortage of power, water, and land
2026-09-21

AI can't outprompt a shortage of power, water, and land

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Forrester predicts operators will face tariffs, grid commitments, and tougher community scrutiny AI infrastructure growth is running into limits on energy, water, and land, according to researchers at Forrester, which predicts governments will increasingly make datacenter operators pay for the resources they consume. Governments, utilities, datacenter operators, and enterprises will have to rethink how that growth is funded, managed, and regulated, the firm said in its 2027 sustainability predictions. The scale of the infrastructure buildout needed to support AI across businesses, governments, and consumer services is beginning to emerge. Earlier this month, PwC put cumulative global datacenter capital expenditure through 2050 at between $22 trillion and $50 trillion, with a central estimate of $31.6 trillion – roughly equivalent to a year of US economic output. Whether grids and communities can support those ambitions is another matter. "Unlimited AI ambition faces a reckoning with limited resources," Forrester said. Forrester predicts that governments and utilities will require datacenter operators to finance grid upgrades, pay for reserved capacity, and provide guarantees against speculative demand. It also expects community impact reviews to become a gate that new projects must pass before construction. Forrester senior analyst Abhijit Sunil explained the reasoning in a blog post: "With unprecedented growth demands, utilities cannot assume that every proposed datacenter will arrive on schedule or consume its promised load. Building generation and transmission for speculative demand could leave households and businesses paying for stranded infrastructure. "Already, the White House has called on hyperscalers to procure their own power, fund upgrades, and pay for reserved capacity even when unused. In 2027, at least two countries will turn this ratepayer-protection principle into tariffs or equivalent obligations. Cloud and colocation costs will become more location-specific, contractually committed, and exposed to power-market risk." Sunil said datacenter operators should expect long-term minimum payment commitments, financial guarantees, energy tariffs, and requirements to reduce their loads when grids are under strain. "Cloud economics will consequently become more dependent on location, electricity contracts, grid maturity, and providers' ability to generate or reduce power. Enterprises rapidly scaling AI capacity will increasingly inherit these costs," he said. The US, however, appears to be moving in the opposite direction on some forms of environmental oversight. In August, the US Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) proposed eliminating federal minimum requirements for public participation in minor-source air permits. The change could make it easier for some datacenter power projects to proceed without public notice, even as Forrester predicts that community scrutiny will increasingly determine which facilities get built. ® Ad biz promises users control while bug could expose voice prompts 'Humans are responsible, not the AI,' argues Scott Bessent as he calls out OpenAI agents' hack of Hugging Face PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity 'A new category,' insists the Chocolate Factory, not a premium Chromebook - yet some of the family resemblance remains War damage, air traffic chaos, and a storage shortage expose the cost of treating resilience as someone else's problem Rent-a-GPU firm reportedly seeks a valuation of up to $35B in New York IPO 'Humans are responsible, not the AI,' argues Scott Bessent as he calls out OpenAI agents' hack of Hugging Face 'A new category,' insists the Chocolate Factory, not a premium Chromebook - yet some of the family resemblance remains Forrester predicts operators will face tariffs, grid commitments, and tougher community scrutiny Seats, Flex Credits, and all-you-can-eat contracts make for an 'anxiety-filled architecture' The Rust compiler remains unperturbed by the antics of the LLM PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' AWS bolts together open source agent harness, says it sips fewer tokens than rivals
2026-09-21

AWS bolts together open source agent harness, says it sips fewer tokens than rivals

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Strands claims near-parity with Claude Code and Codex on benchmarks, though it only raced coding agents and marked its own homework AWS has entered the open source agentic AI game, claiming its new Strands harness matches rivals on benchmarks while using around a quarter fewer tokens. The Strands harness, as its name suggests, is built on AWS’ Strands Harness SDK, but is packaged up and ready to roll out of the box, either locally or deployed to work with whatever AI provider a customer prefers. "The moment you build your own agent, you're on your own," AWS noted in its announcement. "It's tricky wiring up the right primitives just well enough to match that ‘it just worked’ feeling." This new open-source option means that 'all you need is one line of Python or TypeScript to get it working with your choice of model," AWS adds. On top of being relatively plug-and-play in design, AWS claims the Strands harness achieved "nearly equal benchmark scores" versus Claude Code, Codex, and “other popular harnesses” when tested using the Harbor framework, distributed across multiple nodes of AWS’ own EC2 virtual servers for benchmarking tests. Strands consumed 28 percent fewer tokens across six benchmark tests when compared to “Claude or GPT models,” claims AWS, and in some cases had better accuracy than other harnesses too. DeepSeek's harness, AWS admits, was more token efficient but - we're told - was less accurate on the same tests. AWS credits this performance to the Strands harness’ default prompt caching and context management settings. Per the announcement post, it defaults to truncating tool results over 1,500 tokens, automatically compacting its context window when it surpasses 85 percent, and automatically trying to recover context in overflow cases. It’s worth pointing out, however, that AWS describes the Strands harness as being built to be "a general-purpose agent rather than a coding agent." Considering that every agent harness it was compared to in AWS’ benchmarks (Claude Code, Codex, oh-my-pi, OpenCode, and DeepSeek Harness) is billed as a coding agent, it’s worth questioning how other general-purpose AI agents would perform when compared to the new Strands harness. Amazon plans to publish a paper from its researchers with more benchmark details - we’ll keep an eye out to see more of the specifics on how it was evaluated. In the meantime, anyone who wants to give the pre-packaged, open-source, general-purpose Strands harness a shot can do so by using the instructions included in the announcement post. It’s available as a Python or TypeScript library, and also has a CLI. And it ships with a skills file, so plugging in your own preferred tools should be relatively easy; shell, file, and web tools are included at launch. “We believe quickly prototyping with a batteries included harness will create more useful agents,” AWS says of the Strands harness design. It’s live now, so if you’re looking for another open-source option it’s ready when you are, even if a more independent gauge of its performance is currently lacking. ® Ad biz promises users control while bug could expose voice prompts 'Humans are responsible, not the AI,' argues Scott Bessent as he calls out OpenAI agents' hack of Hugging Face PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity 'A new category,' insists the Chocolate Factory, not a premium Chromebook - yet some of the family resemblance remains War damage, air traffic chaos, and a storage shortage expose the cost of treating resilience as someone else's problem Rent-a-GPU firm reportedly seeks a valuation of up to $35B in New York IPO 'Humans are responsible, not the AI,' argues Scott Bessent as he calls out OpenAI agents' hack of Hugging Face 'A new category,' insists the Chocolate Factory, not a premium Chromebook - yet some of the family resemblance remains Forrester predicts operators will face tariffs, grid commitments, and tougher community scrutiny Seats, Flex Credits, and all-you-can-eat contracts make for an 'anxiety-filled architecture' The Rust compiler remains unperturbed by the antics of the LLM PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' RansomHouse picks a fight with Namibia's defense establishment
2026-09-21

RansomHouse picks a fight with Namibia's defense establishment

www.theregister.com - Articles | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
National cyber team confirms the breach, but not whether data was stolen or encrypted Namibia's computer security incident response team has confirmed unauthorized activity in the defense ministry's network and linked it to the RansomHouse cybercrime group. In an unusually direct attribution, NAM-CSIRT named the group after RansomHouse listed the supposed victim on its leak site on September 16. RansomHouse identified the victim as the "Namibian Defence Force," although the domain in its listing belongs to the Ministry of Defence and Veterans Affairs (MODVA), the government department overseeing the military. Either way, openly extorting a country's defense establishment is a bold move even by ransomware standards. RansomHouse's website stated: "Dear management of Namibian Defence Force. We were waiting for you for quite some time, but it seems that your IT department decided to conceal the incident that took place in your company. "We strongly recommend you to contact us to prevent your confidential data, projects documents from being leaked." The listing treated the target as a company with $434 million in annual revenue, but NAM-CSIRT subsequently confirmed unauthorized activity within MODVA's network. NAM-CSIRT said in a statement: "Analysis of the affected systems established that the incident is associated with the RansomHouse ransomware group, a cybercriminal syndicate known internationally for deploying ransomware and engaging in so-called double extortion tactics where threat actors encrypt systems while simultaneously threatening to disclose alleged stolen information. "NAM-CSIRT remains actively engaged in coordinating technical support, investigation activities, remediation measures, and post-incident reviews in line with the National Incident Management framework outlined in the National Cyber Security Incident Management guidelines." Emilia Nghikembua, chief executive of the Communications Regulatory Authority of Namibia and head of NAM-CSIRT, said the team would support MODVA throughout its investigation and recovery. She urged organizations across Namibia to report cyberattacks promptly so authorities can build a fuller picture of the threat landscape. "The collective security of Namibia's digital ecosystem depends on timely reporting, proactive information sharing, and continuous investment in cybersecurity preparedness," she said. "We urge all organizations to strengthen their cyber defences and work closely with NAM-CSIRT to safeguard critical systems, data, and services that citizens depend upon every day." NAM-CSIRT did not answer The Register's questions about the expected recovery timeline, whether a ransom had been demanded, or whether any payment was under consideration. It has not disclosed which systems or data were affected, whether information was stolen, or whether any files were encrypted. RansomHouse is associated with double extortion, in which attackers encrypt systems while threatening to publish stolen data. However, many modern extortion attacks dispense with encryption and rely solely on stolen information as leverage. Active since 2021, RansomHouse is not among the most prolific extortion groups, but it has outlasted many rivals that appeared and disappeared during the same period. According to Halcyon's Ransomware Research Center, the group has listed a steady number of victims each year since it began operating, although it remains less active than dominant operations such as The Gentlemen and Qilin. ® Ad biz promises users control while bug could expose voice prompts 'Humans are responsible, not the AI,' argues Scott Bessent as he calls out OpenAI agents' hack of Hugging Face PARTNER CONTENT: Pairs block and adjacent file workloads with independent scaling of performance and capacity 'A new category,' insists the Chocolate Factory, not a premium Chromebook - yet some of the family resemblance remains War damage, air traffic chaos, and a storage shortage expose the cost of treating resilience as someone else's problem Rent-a-GPU firm reportedly seeks a valuation of up to $35B in New York IPO 'Humans are responsible, not the AI,' argues Scott Bessent as he calls out OpenAI agents' hack of Hugging Face 'A new category,' insists the Chocolate Factory, not a premium Chromebook - yet some of the family resemblance remains Forrester predicts operators will face tariffs, grid commitments, and tougher community scrutiny Seats, Flex Credits, and all-you-can-eat contracts make for an 'anxiety-filled architecture' The Rust compiler remains unperturbed by the antics of the LLM PLUS: US takes down Iranian propaganda sites; Marketing company asks 'Why Do We Have Your Information?' GM can’t ‘bring back’ Apple CarPlay because it never left
2026-09-21

GM can’t ‘bring back’ Apple CarPlay because it never left

The Verge | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
The recent reveal of GM’s new software interface in its trucks has left some people confused about the automaker’s stance on phone mirroring. The recent reveal of GM’s new software interface in its trucks has left some people confused about the automaker’s stance on phone mirroring. Last week, GM announced a new software experience for its upcoming Chevy Silverado and GMC Sierra trucks, including a new look for Apple CarPlay and Android Auto, the popular phone mirroring systems that the automaker made a big deal about removing from its electric vehicles in 2023. Naturally, this led to some confusion about whether GM was “bringing back” CarPlay and Android Auto to its vehicles. Yahoo Finance reported it that way, calling it an “about-face” by the automaker, while Apple Insider’s headline boasted that GM “cave[d]” on its decision to remove the interface from its gas-powered vehicles. Even The Verge’s own commenters were a bit flummoxed about what was going on. It’s certainly true that GM has not offered phone projection in any of its electric vehicles for the past three years. That means no CarPlay or Android Auto in the Chevy Equinox, Blazer, Bolt, and Silverado EVs; Cadillac Lyriq, Optiq, Vistiq, and Escalade IQ; or GMC Sierra and Hummer SUV and Truck EVs. All of those vehicles have Google software products embedded in their systems, like Maps and Gemini. That’s because GM builds its operating system on top of Android Automotive, which is often called Google Automotive Services or GAS when bundled with the full suite of native apps. But if you’re hoping to plug in your phone, or connect wirelessly, and see the familiar app layout on the infotainment screen of a GM electric vehicle, you’ll be sorely disappointed. The same can’t be said about GM’s many gas-powered trucks and SUVs. GM never disabled phone projection in its best-selling ICE models. Tens of thousands of owners of GM-branded vehicles regularly project their phones’ displays on their vehicles’ infotainment screens without any issue. And now CarPlay and Android Auto are both getting spiffy new picture-in-picture upgrades in the Chevy Silverado and GMC Sierra, a sign that perhaps GM is learning to co-exist with phone mirroring. GM is partly responsible for the confusion. In an October 2025 appearance on Decoder, GM CEO Mary Barra defended the company’s decision to prohibit CarPlay in its electric vehicles and indicated that the policy would eventually extend to GM’s gasoline-powered lineup as well. Everyone freaked out for a second time. Removing phone projection in EVs was one thing, but many truck and SUV owners were understandably incensed about the prospect of losing the capability in their beloved GM-branded vehicles. But a few days later, GM seemed to walk back that statement, clarifying to a number of outlets that Apple CarPlay and Android Auto would remain available in its gas-powered vehicles “for the foreseeable future.” Last week’s announcement of a new software experience in the Silverado and Sierra trucks would seem to be included in that “foreseeable future.” Allow me to add some further reporting here. For the Silverado/Sierra announcement, I spoke to Colin McCormick, group product manager for Google built-in, projection, and profiles at GM. Here’s what he had to say about how GM’s position on phone projection has evolved over the years: And so the way that I look at this is that we were one of the early adopters of CarPlay and Android Auto when it first came out. And we do have a small segment of EVs right now that don’t offer that, but there is a large majority of our vehicles that do offer that. And our big position on this is that it shouldn’t be two competing systems. So as we work together with Apple and Google to provide solutions that we think are best for the customer, this is what we’re representing in this new UI design. So I think we’re landing in a spot where … we don’t have a customer that has to decide, ‘Oh, I’m going to use projection,’ or, ‘Oh, I want to use the native system.’ That’s our sweet spot for us. And that’s where we want to live. GM most likely realizes that its position on CarPlay and Android Auto isn’t popular. People really like mirroring their phones! It’s clean, it’s familiar, and it’s (mostly) dependable. The same can’t be said for most modern cars’ native software. Even the ones that are really well designed, like Rivian and Tesla, still struggle with some core functions like profile logins and messages. And you can find plenty of owners of both brands that would give their firstborn child for the ability to mirror their phone. CarPlay and Android Auto aren’t perfect. They have dependability problems and connectivity problems aplenty. But they are worlds better than the majority of the native operating systems in cars today — most of which are just trash. Yes, they’re getting better. And GM is not wrong in thinking it can offer a better experience. But people get testy when you take away their choices. So GM is finding ways to coexist with CarPlay and Android Auto. It doesn’t want to lose more customers over it, which is why we’re getting this most recent solution that includes picture-in-picture phone projection. GM thinks this solves the “two competing systems” problem, as mentioned by McCormick. But what it really does is get it out of the corner it backed itself into over phone mirroring. Paramount settles lawsuit blocking $110 billion Warner Bros. merger
2026-09-21

Paramount settles lawsuit blocking $110 billion Warner Bros. merger

The Verge | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
This settlement will resolve the antitrust lawsuit filed by California, New York, Washington, and several other states to block Paramount’s big deal. This settlement will resolve the antitrust lawsuit filed by California, New York, Washington, and several other states to block Paramount’s big deal. Paramount has reached a settlement with California and the 11 other states that sued to block its planned $110 billion acquisition of Warner Bros. Discovery. The settlement removes a major roadblock standing in the way of the massive media merger. According to the proposed consent decree filed with the court, it includes requirements like minimum numbers of theatrical film releases over the next five years, starting with 30 in year one and two, and 32 in the following years, and a commitment to spend at least $300 million more on production in the US compared to what the companies spent in 2025. At least four of the films produced must be independent films, and at least 20 percent must be “tentpole” blockbuster movies with $50 million-plus budgets, and releases on a minimum of 3,000 US screens within the first month. If Paramount doesn’t meet these film release requirements, it must divest Miramax Studios and pay $30 million per missed film. It also requires the merged company to keep offering a free streaming service, like Pluto TV, and includes a commitment not to sell Paramount Studios or Warner Bros. lots in California for at least five years. The settlement has rules for the negotiations over cable channels owned by the combined company as well, in addition to requirements for a news editorial independence board “made up of five established journalists” with at least ten years of experience to ensure editorial independence at CBS and CNN. The Writers Guild of America also settled its antitrust lawsuit against Paramount. At a press conference announcing the agreement, California AG Rob Bonta called the arrangement “the opposite” of the 2018 Disney-Fox deal, where Fox’s film output dropped afterward. “Let me be clear: This settlement is not a vote of support for this merger,” Bonta adds in a press release. “But we believe this settlement, which resolves our antitrust concerns in every market alleged in our case, protects competition and consumer choice, and puts workers’ needs, concerns, and futures first, is the best course of action.” We are grateful to Attorney General Bonta and his fellow AGs, as well as the WGA, for engaging in good faith to find a path forward to a resolution that serves all parties, and to Governor Newsom for his support throughout this process. Our shared aim was an outcome that best serves consumers, workers and — most importantly — the creative community so vital to the art of visual storytelling. We’re confident this agreement does exactly that, memorializing a series of commitments that include 30+ films annually and expanded U.S. film production to help revitalize our industry here at home. Our goal has always been to build a stronger Hollywood — one with more stories told, greater choice for consumers and stronger competition. That vision was validated by unanimous approval from competition authorities in nearly 70 jurisdictions worldwide, who agreed this deal is pro-competitive, pro-consumer and pro-worker. Having now addressed the State AGs’ and WGA’s concerns, we have complete clearance for this merger and look forward to putting these commitments into action. Bringing Paramount and Warner Bros. Discovery together will build that stronger Hollywood, creating expanded opportunity for our people and even more great entertainment for audiences around the world. The settlement comes just days before Paramount’s “ticking fee” was set to go into effect, which would require the entertainment giant to pay WBD shareholders 25 cents per share for each quarter the deal is delayed past September 30th. The fee would’ve cost Paramount about $7 million per day. Though the Trump administration signed off on the $110 billion merger, California, along with Arizona, Colorado, New York, Washington, and others, sued Paramount and WBD in July over antitrust concerns. They claimed the megamerger would create a “media behemoth” that would “inflict substantial harm” on the entertainment industry. A judge granted the states’ request to pause the merger shortly after. Bungie says it’s ‘not done with Destiny’ and will bring back vaulted content

The Verge | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Bungie is reversing its controversial content vault that saw Destiny 2 content removed six years ago. Bungie is reversing its controversial content vault that saw Destiny 2 content removed six years ago. Months after seemingly giving up on Destiny 2, Bungie now says it’s planning to restore vaulted content in the game like campaigns, destinations, and raids. The decision reverses the Destiny Content Vault that Bungie created six years ago, controversially removing older content from Destiny 2 that players had paid to access. “We understand that removing these experiences had a lasting impact on player trust and enjoyment of Destiny,” admits Bungie. “We cannot change the decision we made, but we can acknowledge its impact and do the work to make it right by restoring these important parts of Destiny’s history.“ All Destiny 2 players will get access to the vaulted content, but it’s not a simple switch of a button for Bungie. The developer made big changes to Destiny 2 with the Beyond Light expansion, meaning it has to go back and update lighting, encounters, combatants, and other underlying systems for this older content. “That work will take time, but it is necessary to ensure returning content meets the stability and gameplay standards of Destiny 2 today,” says Bungie. The decision to return this content to Destiny 2 comes just weeks after a “Sunrise” Destiny 2 offline preservation mod appeared. The mod lets players access older areas in Destiny 2 that had been vaulted, but not the exact campaigns and missions that players had experienced previously. Bungie now says its own restoration project is the “first step to lay the foundation for how we reforge into new stories and games that you all deserve.” Could that be a Destiny 3 sequel? There’s no mention of such a project, despite fans desperately pleading for a successor to Destiny 2 in multiple high-profile Twitch streams in recent weeks. Bungie is only committing to building a “stronger long-term future for Destiny” right now. Alongside the Destiny 2 efforts, Bungie is also admitting that Marathon, its extraction shooter, didn’t “reach as many players as we had hoped, and we also know and understand that the game needs to evolve.” That means Marathon will expand deeper into a survival experience. The changes are a little vague right now, but it sounds like Marathon really needs to change and attract more players if it’s to be a sustainable project for Bungie. Can John Ternus find Apple’s next big thing?

Can John Ternus find Apple’s next big thing?

The Verge | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
Bloomberg’s chief Apple correspondent Mark Gurman on Siri, Tim Cook’s legacy, and what to expect from new CEO John Ternus. Today, I’m talking with Mark Gurman, who is the world’s best-sourced Apple reporter — both as Bloomberg’s chief Apple correspondent and the host of the upcoming podcast Power On. Earlier this month, Apple hosted its annual iPhone hardware event, virtually every detail of which was reported ahead of time by Mark. But this event was special, for a number of reasons. It was new Apple CEO John Ternus’ first major product launch since he took over for former CEO Tim Cook less than two weeks prior. It was also the debut of the iPhone Duo, Apple’s first foldable smartphone. The timing of those two things was not at all coincidental, as you’ll hear Mark explain. Apple takes these hardware events — and the stories they tell — extremely seriously. This year’s story actually says a great deal about what’s happening at Apple, how Cook managed in wholesale leadership ranks over the past year, and how all of that might change Apple’s culture. Furthermore, what all that tells about Ternus’ plan to navigate Apple from what you might call the iPhone era into the AI era. Okay: Bloomberg’s Mark Gurman on the future of Apple. Here we go. This interview has been lightly edited for length and clarity. Mark Gurman, you’re the chief Apple correspondent and the managing editor of consumer technology at Bloomberg. Welcome to Decoder. Good to be here finally. Thanks for having me. And it’s been a while, so it’s good to catch up on all things Apple. Yeah, I’m really excited to talk to you. Obviously, Apple just had its big event. They announced the iPhone Duo. It was John Ternus’s first event as CEO. This is a Decoder episode, so we’ve got a new CEO, and we’ve got a new org chart inside of Apple. There’s been a lot of shuffling. You’ve been chronicling them very, very deeply over the past few years. Let’s just start with the event. I have a very basic question for you. Do you ever get nostalgic for a time when you didn’t know what was going to happen at the iPhone event? Do you ever wish that you, Mark Gurman, were about to be surprised? Honestly, I never think about it that way. I’m just fixated on delivering the goods and delivering the news. And people love their Apple. People want to know everything that’s coming down to the last minute, so I’m here to provide it for them. The thing with me, and I don’t know if most people know this about me, but I hope people know this about me, is that I’m a huge Apple fan through and through, and I’ve always been. And I’m so lucky to be able to cover a company that I love, a company that I am obsessed with, and a company whose products I use. There are so many people who cover companies that they couldn’t care less about. That’s not me. So I do what I do because I love Apple and I love covering Apple. I love the community around it, and I love talking about it. And it’s always fun to get a peek at the future and help keep not only myself informed, but people informed around me. That actually leads me to a lot of things because I’ve known you forever. We’ve been working in and around the same spaces forever. You got your start as an Apple blogger at the Apple rumor sites. There’s a whole culture around Apple that has grown up and changed over the years, and you can trace it to different eras of hardware, to different CEOs, to different sizes of the company. This was the first event with John Ternus as CEO. Apple announced a big new product in the iPhone Duo. Do you sense that the community is changing? How do you think that community has changed over the years? And do you think Ternus is going to lead to a difference in what that community feels like? On the other hand, I really don’t think they need to be that worried because if it’s a success, they can just copy it, which is, I guess, what they’re accusing OpenAI of doing from the beginning anyway. In terms of the lawsuit, it is very clear that OpenAI employees have done some shady things. I’m surprised that the iPhone engineer, or former iPhone engineer, is at the center of the lawsuit; this is the one they’ve accused of taking material with him and sharing it with people and whatnot. I’m shocked that OpenAI has not cut bait on him and moved forward. I think their fear is that if they cut bait on this guy, employees are going to revolt. How could we trust working at a company like this if you are going to get sued by Apple, even a company that big, and you’re just going to cut bait on someone, right? So we’ll see what happens. We’ll see what happens. They’ve done shady things, but a lot of the stuff, as you said, is common business practice, stuff that Apple probably even does too. It’s very notable to me that none of the filings in the case so far have any mention of Jony Ive. Neither company wants to point out that Steve Jobs’ number two is at the center of all this because he’s leading the design of this product. I think it’s by design. I think it’s notable. And I even think the origin story of this lawsuit is Jony Ive himself. I think Apple’s point of view is that Jony Ive was on Apple’s payroll for half a decade or more after the Apple Watch. During that time, basically destroyed the Apple design team from within and took everyone with him to build a competitor to the products that he developed at Apple. That is Apple’s viewpoint. I think there’s a lot of anger towards Ive at the very center of this, particularly from Tim Cook and some of those other players there. I think Jony Ive’s perspective is that all the Apple products today are direct siblings of everything Jony Ive developed there. Even the iPhone Duo, you can see Jony Ive’s work in there. It’s all part and parcel with Jony Ive. I think that he believes that he created the product portfolio that’s the basis of this $3 to 4 trillion company. And he was tired after carrying that company post-Steve Jobs and very sad about Steve Jobs’ death, and wanted to move on. He and Tim Cook came to an agreement that he only needed to work at Apple for a few days a week. And so what? If he wants to eventually leave Apple and build something new and have people follow him, that’s up to them. So I think it’s two very different sides of the same story, and you can side with whoever you want to side with. But I think it’s really unfortunate that things did not work out for Apple and for Jony Ive together in this instance. I think he saw AI as the new core technology that he can develop something really cool around, just like the iPhone was developed around the cool technologies back then of the App Store, Multi-Touch, and what have you. What’s really interesting about that, and this is a whole different podcast, is that all of Ive’s best products are designed around limitations. The iMac was designed around the limitation of the CRT display. AI doesn’t have any limitations because it always just tells you it can do everything. I’m just dying to know what he identifies as the limitation that makes the magic of the next Ive product. As I said, that’s a whole different podcast, but it’s just on my mind as I think about what he might do to make this more than just a hockey puck that’s listening all the time. I’m psyched to play with it and to see it. And I think this screenless idea is both a blessing and a curse. I think that we’re going to really see how effective AI is when we’re using it without a screen and having it in the background. But at the same time, there are things about displays that are not replaceable with screenless devices. Gaming, massive market. Video conferencing, massive use case. Watching video, another massive market use case. I just don’t know how OpenAI takes over the world with a device that can’t video chat, can’t do gaming, and can’t do video. And we’ve seen historically that being an iPhone companion, that just doesn’t cut it. Actually, this brings me to you mentioning the AirPods with a camera. AirPods are a success because they have a huge attach rate to iPhones. People buy iPhones; they buy AirPods. People buy iPhones; they buy a set of AirPods. There you go. You got a huge business. And then you lose them every six months, or you break them, and you have to buy new ones. So it’s crazy. You mentioned that adding a camera to AirPods might turn them into a platform unto themselves. That would obviously compete with some of these screenless devices or these AI native devices. How would that work? Okay. These AirPods basically have the visual intelligence feature that you have on your iPhone. You could point at something. You can take action on it. You can get more information. It’s built into the AirPods. It’s no different. So one use case is that I’m on my computer, and I have my phone, and I want to add something to my calendar. iPhone owners can now submit claims in Apple’s $250 million Siri AI settlement
2026-09-21

iPhone owners can now submit claims in Apple’s $250 million Siri AI settlement

The Verge | 2026-09-21

Visit Original Website
The deadline to file a claim is December 21st, 2026. The deadline to file a claim is December 21st, 2026. Apple is paying $250 million to settle claims that it failed to deliver an AI-upgraded Siri — and now, eligible iPhone owners can submit a claim for a payout. If you live in the US and purchased an iPhone 15 Pro, iPhone 15 Pro Max, or any iPhone 16 model between June 10th, 2024, and March 29th, 2025, you can file a claim on the settlement’s website, as reported earlier by MacRumors. You can receive an estimated $25 per eligible device, but the settlement’s website notes that this can increase to $95 depending on how many people file claims. To file a claim, you’ll need to enter your name, contact information, and iPhone serial number on the settlement’s website before the December 21st, 2026 deadline. The class action lawsuit alleged Apple created a “clear and reasonable consumer expectation” that new AI features previewed during WWDC 2024 would launch alongside the iPhone 16. Instead, the iPhone 16 launched with a more limited set of capabilities despite being labeled as “built for Apple Intelligence.” The company later delayed the rollout of its AI-upgraded Siri, which only just arrived with iOS 27 earlier this month. Apple has denied any wrongdoing. A free daily digest of the news that matters most. Sign in to see your notifications or create an account to join the conversation.
